Timeout Asynchronous Session Types: Safe Asynchronous Mixed-Choice for Timed Interactions Thanks: This work has been partially supported by EPSRC project EP/T014512/1 (STARDUST) and the BehAPI project funded by the EU H2020 RISE under the Marie Sklodowska-Curie action (No: 778233). We thank Simon Thompson for his insightful comments on an early version of this work.
Jonah Pears ORCID:0000-0003-4492-4072, Laura Bocchi ORCID:0000-0002-7177-9395, Maurizio Murgia ORCID:0000-0001-7613-621X, Andy King ORCID:0000-0001-5806-4822

Abstract
. Mixed-choice has long been barred from models of asynchronous communication since it compromises the decidability of key properties of communicating finite-state machines. Session types inherit this restriction, which precludes them from fully modelling timeouts – a core property of web and cloud services. To address this deficiency, we present (binary) Timeout Asynchronous Session Types (TOAST) as an extension to (binary) asynchronous timed session types, that permits mixed-choice. TOAST deploys timing constraints to regulate the use of mixed-choice so as to preserve communication safety. We provide a new behavioural semantics for TOAST which guarantees progress in the presence of mixed-choice. Building upon TOAST, we provide a calculus featuring process timers which is capable of modelling timeouts using a 𝚛𝚎𝚌𝚎𝚒𝚟𝚎 ​ - ​ 𝚊𝚏𝚝𝚎𝚛 \mathtt{receive\text{-}after} pattern, much like Erlang, and capture the correspondence with TOAST specifications via a type system for which we prove subject reduction.

This work is an extended version of
[
PBK23
]
that was accepted at COORDINATION 2023.

1.
Introduction

Mixed-choice is an inherent feature of models of communications such as communicating finite-state machines (CFSM)
[
BZ83
]
where actions are classified as either send or receive. In this setting, a state of a machine is said to be mixed if there exist both a sending action and a receiving action from that state.
When considering an asynchronous model of communication, deadlock freedom is undecidable in general
[
GMY84
]
but can be guaranteed in presence of three sufficient and decidable conditions: determinism, compatibility, and
absence
of mixed-states
[
GMY84
,
DY13
]
. Intuitively, determinism means that it is not possible, from a state, to reach two different states with the same kind of action, and compatibility requires that for each send action of one machine, the rest of the system can eventually perform a complementary receive action.

In the pursuit of deadlock freedom mixed-choice has been cast out,
even though this curtails
the
descriptive capabilities
of CFSM and its derivatives.
Despite the rapid evolution of session types,
even to the point of deployment in
Java
[
HYH08
]
,
Python
[
NYH13
,
Ney13
]
,
Rust
[
LNY20
]
,
F#
[
NHYA18
]

and Go
[
CHJ+19
]
,
thus far mixed-choice has only been introduced into the
synchronous binary setting
[
VCAM20
]
.
In fact, the exclusion of mixed-choice pervades work on asynchronous communication which guarantee of deadlock freedom,
both for
communicating timed automata
[
BLY15
,
KY06
]

and session types
[
BCD+08
,
CHY08
,
HYC08
,
YV07
]
.
Determinism and the absence of mixed-choice is baked into the very syntax of session types
(the correspondence between session types and
CFSM is explained in
[
DY13
]
).

Timed session types
[
BCP14
,
BMVY19
,
BYY14
]
, which extend session types with time constraints, inherit the same syntactic restrictions of session types, and hence also rule out mixed-choice.
This is unfortunate since in the timed setting, mixed-choice are a useful abstraction for timeouts.
To illustrate,
Figure

1
shows how
an Erlang
[
CV16
]

\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
statement (
1A
)
can be rendered as a mixed-state CFSM (
1B
):
if neither a
data
nor a
done
message are received within 3 time units from co-party
Q
,
then a
timeout
message is sent.

Timeouts are important for handling failure and unexpected delays, for instance, the SMTP protocol stipulates:

“An SMTP client
must
provide a timeout mechanism”

[
Kle08
, Section 4.5.3.2]
.
Mixed-states would allow, for example, states where a server is waiting to
receive
a message from the client and, if nothing is received after a certain amount of time,
send
a notification that ends the session.
Current variants of timed session types allow deadlines to be expressed but cannot, because of the absence of mixed-states, characterise (and verify) the behaviour that should follow a missed deadline, e.g., a restart or retry strategy.
In this paper, we argue that time makes mixed-states more powerful (allowing timeouts to be expressed), while just adding sufficient synchronisation to ensure that mixed-states are safe in an asynchronous semantics (cannot produce deadlocks).

⬇

1

receive

2

{
Q
,

data
}

->

foo
();

3

{
Q
,

done
}

->

bar
();

4

after

3000

->

5

Q

!

{
self
(),

timeout
},

6

handle_timeout
()

7

end
.

A

Erlang snippet

ra
f
b
t
?data
(x<3)
?done
(x<3)
!timeout
(x\geq 3)

B

Mixed-state CFSM

Figure 1
.
An Erlang snippet and its mixed-state machine representation

Contributions

This work makes three orthogonal contributions to the theory of (binary) session types, with a focus on improving their descriptive capabilities:

•

We introduce Timeout Asynchronous Session Types (TOAST) to support timeouts.
Inspired by asynchronous timed (binary) session types
[
BMVY19
]
, TOAST shows how timing constraints provide an elegant solution for guaranteeing the safety of mixed-choice. Technically, we provide a semantics for TOAST and a well-formedness condition. We show that well-formedness is sufficient to guarantee progress for TOAST (which may, instead, get stuck in general).

•

We provide a new process calculus whose functionality extends to support programming motifs such as the widely used
\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
pattern of Erlang for expressing timeouts.

•

We introduce timers in our process calculus to structure the counterpart of a timeout, as well as time-sensitive conditional statements, where the selection of a branch may be determined by timers. Time-sensitive conditional statements provide processes with knowledge as to which branch should be followed e.g., in the case of timeout.

•

We provide an informal discussion on the correspondence between TOAST and the aforementioned primitives of our new process calculus.

•

We show a formal correspondence between TOAST and process calculus via a typing system, for which we establish Subject Reduction.

For simplicity, we focus on binary sessions.

Outline

In
Section

2
we begin by introducing Timeout Asynchronous Session Types and present their syntax, semantics,
and discuss mixed-choice and other motivations behind our well-formedness conditions.
We end
Section

2
by discussing our proof of progress for TOAST.

In
Section

3
we present a calculus for processes with timeouts, which are designed to correspond to TOAST, and discuss their syntax, structure and reduction rules.

Section

4
discusses the expressiveness of our types and processes, and provides some examples of the kinds of programs that TOAST is capable of modelling, and vice versa, how to express certain behaviour in TOAST and our process calculus.

We present our typing system in
Section

5
.
We first introduce the typing judgements, then discuss each rule in detail and conclude with an example evaluation
using our typing system.
In
Section

6
we present subject reduction for our typing system.
We conclude our work in
Sections

7
and
8
, where we discuss related work and summarise our contribution.

2.
Timeout Asynchronous Session Types (TOAST)

This section presents the syntax, semantics and formation rules for (binary) Timeout Asynchronous Session Types (TOAST),
which are an extension of
(binary) Asynchronous Timed Session Types
[
BMVY19
]
with a well-disciplined
(and hence safe)
form of mixed-choice.

Clocks & Constraints

We start with a few preliminary definitions borrowed from timed automata
[
AD94
]
. Let
\MathOpSetOfClocks
be a finite set of clocks denoted
x
,
y
and
z
.
A (clock) valuation
{\nu}
is a map
{\nu}:\MathOpSetOfClocks\to\MathOpSetRealZ
.
The initial valuation is
{\nu}_{0}
, where

{\nu}_{0}=\{x\mapsto 0\mid x\in\MathOpSetOfClocks\}
.
Given a time offset

{t}\in\MathOpSetRealZ

and a valuation
{\nu}
,

{\nu}+{t}=\{x\mapsto{\nu}(x)+{t}\mid x\in\MathOpSetOfClocks\}
.
Given
{\nu}
and
{\lambda}\subseteq\MathOpSetOfClocks
,

{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]=\{0\ \text{if\ }(x\in{\lambda})\text{\ else\ }{\nu}(x)\mid x\in\MathOpSetOfClocks\}
.
Observe
{\nu}\left[{{\varnothing}\mapsto 0}\right]={\nu}
.

A clock constraint
\delta
takes the following form:
[]

{\delta}::=\MathOpTrue\mid{x>n}\mid{x=n}\mid{x-y>n}\mid{x-y=n}\mid{\neg{\delta}}\mid{{\delta}_{1}\land{\delta}_{2}}\qquad(\text{where\ }n\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0})

(2.1)

We write
{\nu}\models\delta
to denote that the clock valuations in
\nu
satisfy the constraints
\delta
.
Defined formally,

({\nu}\models\delta)=(\forall x\in\mathtt{cn}({\delta}).\delta\left[{\nicefrac{{\nu(x)}}{{x}}}\right])

to say that, for all clocks in the

clock names
1
1

1

Given in
Section

A.1
.

of
\delta

we substitute the clock name with the corresponding valuation in
\nu
.
E.g.,

\nu[x\mapsto 3]\models(x>2)
holds since
(x>2)\left[{\nicefrac{{\nu(x)}}{{x}}}\right]=(3>2)
.
We write
\downarrow{{\delta}}
to denote the
weak past
of

\delta
.

[Weak Past]
We write
\downarrow{{\delta}}
(the past of
{\delta}
)
for a constraint
{\delta}^{\prime}

such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}^{\prime}
,
if and only if
\exists{t}

such that
{\nu}+{t}\models{\delta}
.

By
Section

2
,
\downarrow{{\delta}}
effectively removes any lower-bounds in
\delta
, preserving the upper-bounds.
For example,

\downarrow(3<x<5)=(x<5)

and
\downarrow(x>2)=(\MathOpTrue)
.
In practice

\downarrow{{\delta}}
allows us to reason on constraints being satisfiable at some point in the future,
since we only require that the clocks valuation does not exceed the upper-bound of the constraint.

2.1.
Syntax of TOAST

The syntax of TOAST (or just
types
) is given in
Equation

2.2
.
A type
{S}
is either a choice
\left\{{{\mathtt{C}}_{i}}\right\}_{{i}\in I}
,
recursive definition
{\mu\alpha.{S}}
,
call
\alpha
,
or termination type
\mathtt{end}
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{lcl @{\qquad} lcl}{S}&::=&\left\{{{\mathtt{C}}_{i}}\right\}_{{i}\in I}\mid{\mu\alpha.{S}}\mid\alpha\mid\mathtt{end}&\mathtt{C}&::=&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}\,{{l}}\left\langle{{T}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}},{{\lambda}}}\right)}.{{S}}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
{T}&::=&\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta},\penalty\ {S}\end{array}\mid\mathtt{Unit}\mid\mathtt{Nat}\mid\mathtt{Bool}\mid\mathtt{String}\mid\dots&\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}&::=&{!\mathstrut}\mid{?\mathstrut}\end{array}

(2.2)

Type
\left\{{{\mathtt{C}}_{i}}\right\}_{{i}\in I}
models a choice among options
i
ranging over a non-empty set
I
. Each option
i
is a selection/send action if
\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}=!
, or alternatively a branching/receive action if
\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}=?
.
An option sends (resp. receives) a label
{l}
and a message of a specified data type
{T}
is delineated by
\langle\cdot\rangle
.
The sending or receiving action of an option is guarded by a time constraint
{\delta}
. After the action, the clocks within
{\lambda}
are reset to 0.
Data types, ranged over by
{T},{T}_{i},\ldots

can be higher-order types
\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta},\penalty\ {S}\end{array}
to model session delegation,

\mathtt{Unit}
types, or
base types
(e.g.,
\mathtt{Nat}
,
\mathtt{Bool}
,
\mathtt{String}
). We omit a
\mathtt{Unit}
type when the payload is nothing.
We discuss the structure of delegation types further in
Section

2.3.1
.
Labels of the options in a choice are pairwise distinct.
Recursion and termination types are as standard
[
BCD+08
,
HYC08
,
Vas12
,
YV07
]
.

Remarks on the notation

One convention is to model the exchange of payloads as a separated action with respect to the communication of branching labels.
In this paper we follow
[
BLY15
,
YZF21
]
, and model them as unique actions.
When irrelevant we omit the payload, yielding a notation closer to that of timed automata.

Feasibility & Junk Types

Unfortunately, when annotating session types with time constraints one may obtain protocols that are infeasible, as shown in
Section

2.1
.
This is a known problem, which has been addressed by providing additional conditions or constraints on timed session types, for example compliance
[
BCM17
]
, feasibility
[
BYY14
]
, interaction enabling
[
BLY15
]
, and well-formedness
[
BMVY19
]
.
(We address feasibility in
Section

2.3.1
.)

[Junk Types]
Consider type
{S}
(defined below) that describes first waiting 3 seconds, then receiving
a
, and finally a mixed-choice between sending
b
and receiving
c
.

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}{S}&=&{?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x>3},{\varnothing}\right).{\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({y=2},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}{,}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{c}{}\left({2<x<5},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}\end{array}}\end{array}

Initially all clocks are 0.
After
a
is received all clocks hold values greater than 3,
since time passes evenly over all clocks.
Therefore,
b
is never able to be sent since
(y>3)

and
c
is only
sometimes
able to be sent
(i.e., if
a
is received when
(x<5)
which constraint
(x>3)
does nothing to guarantee).
Types with unsatisfiable constraints are called
junk types

[
BMVY19
]
.

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}{S}^{\prime}&=&{?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x>3},{\left\{x\right\}}\right).{\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({y=2},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}{,}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{c}{}\left({2<x<5},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}\end{array}}\end{array}

Above we show an amended
{S}
which now resets clock
x
after receiving
a
in order to guarantee that receiving
c
is always feasible.
By further amending
{S}^{\prime}
such that clock
y
is also reset after receiving
a
then sending
b
would also become feasible.

\triangle

2.2.
Semantics of TOAST

We define the semantics
using three layers:
configurations
(
{\mathbf{s}}
),
configurations with queues
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
that model asynchronous interactions
(
{\mathbf{S}}
),
and systems that model the parallel composition of configurations with queues.
The semantics, for all layers, are defined over the transition labels
\ell
,
formally defined along with
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
,
{\mathbf{s}}
and
{\mathbf{S}}
in
Equation

2.3
.
[]

\ell::={\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}\mid{t}\mid\tau\qquad{m}::={{l}\left\langle{{T}}\right\rangle}\qquad\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}::={\varnothing\mid{m};\,\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}}\qquad{\mathbf{s}}::=({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\qquad{\mathbf{S}}::=({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})

(2.3)

where
transition label

\ell

is
either
an interaction
(\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m})
,
time-step (
t\in\MathOpSetRealZ
)
or silent action
(\tau)
,
and
{m}
is a message and
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
is a FIFO message queue.
Communication directions

\square

are the same as in
Equation

2.2
,
where
!
denotes a send/output action
and
?
a receive/input action.

2.2.1.
Configurations

A configuration
{\mathbf{s}}
is a pair
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
.
The semantics for configurations are defined by a Labelled Transition System (LTS) over configurations, the labels in
Equation

2.3

and the rules given
in
Equation

2.4
.
A transition
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}\penalty\ {\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
indicates
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
, where
{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
is some intermediate configuration.
Changes from
[
BMVY19
]
are
highlighted
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\hbox to389.54pt{\vbox to14.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.53 L 0 15.24 C 0 17.74 2.03 19.77 4.53 19.77 L 534.47 19.77 C 536.97 19.77 539 17.74 539 15.24 L 539 4.53 C 539 2.03 536.97 0 534.47 0 L 4.53 0 C 2.03 0 0 2.03 0 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.57 4.53 L 1.57 15.24 C 1.57 16.87 2.9 18.19 4.53 18.19 L 534.47 18.19 C 536.1 18.19 537.42 16.87 537.42 15.24 L 537.42 4.53 C 537.42 2.9 536.1 1.57 534.47 1.57 L 4.53 1.57 C 2.9 1.57 1.57 2.9 1.57 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.82863pt}{4.64311pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{72}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope

\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c @{\qquad} c}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}\left[{\nicefrac{{{\mu\alpha.{S}}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}})\quad\left[{\mathtt{{tick}}}\right]\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

(2.4)

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
a configuration may perform
one action
j\in I
, provided the corresponding
\delta_{j}
is satisfied by the current valuation of clocks
{\nu}
.
The resulting configuration has all clocks in
{\lambda}_{j}
reset to 0.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{tick}}}\right]
describes time passing.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{unfold}}}\right]
unfolds recursive types.

[Future-enabled Configurations]

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
future-enabled
,
written either as
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}}{{\Rightarrow}}
or
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\text{\ is\ }\mathtt{fe}
,
iff
\exists{t}

such that
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}\penalty\ {\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}}$}}\,
via the rules
in
Equation

2.4
.

2.2.2.
Configurations with queues

A configuration with queues
{\mathbf{S}}
is a triple
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})
, where
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
is a FIFO queue of messages which have been received but not yet processed.
A queue takes the form
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}::=\varnothing\mid{m};\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
and thus is either empty, or has a message at its head.
The semantics of configurations with queues is defined by an LTS over the labels in
Equation

2.3
and the rules
in
Equation

2.5
.
The transition
{\mathbf{S}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}\penalty\ {\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
is defined analogously to
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}\penalty\ {\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{c @{\qquad} c}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ {m};\,\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})\end{array}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}};\,{m}})\quad\left[{\mathtt{{que}}}\right]\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
\hbox to450.34pt{\vbox to38.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.53 L 0 48.24 C 0 50.74 2.03 52.77 4.53 52.77 L 618.6 52.77 C 621.1 52.77 623.13 50.74 623.13 48.24 L 623.13 4.53 C 623.13 2.03 621.1 0 618.6 0 L 4.53 0 C 2.03 0 0 2.03 0 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.57 4.53 L 1.57 48.24 C 1.57 49.87 2.9 51.19 4.53 51.19 L 618.6 51.19 C 620.23 51.19 621.55 49.87 621.55 48.24 L 621.55 4.53 C 621.55 2.9 620.23 1.57 618.6 1.57 L 4.53 1.57 C 2.9 1.57 1.57 2.9 1.57 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.82863pt}{16.56664pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{72}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

(2.5)

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{send}}}\right]
emits a message
{m}
.
Message reception is handled by two rules: rule
\left[{\mathtt{{que}}}\right]
inserts a message at the back of
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
, and rule
\left[{\mathtt{{recv}}}\right]
removes a message from the front of
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
.

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
is for time passing which is formulated in terms of a future-enabled configuration, given in
Section

2.2.1
.
The second condition in the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]

(persistency)
ensures the “latest-enabled” action is never missed by advancing the clocks.
Notably, this differs from the corresponding behaviour in
[
BMVY19
]
which, in the presence of mixed-choice, is too restrictive.
By not allowing a participant to pass its last sending action, the latest-enabled receiving actions can never be reached (i.e., miss sending to receive a timeout).
The third condition
(urgency)
models an urgent semantics, ensuring messages are processed as they arrive.
Urgency is critical for reasoning about progress.
Later, in
Section

2.3.3
we further discuss the changes made to rule
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
and illustrate why they are necessary.

2.2.3.
Systems

Systems are the parallel composition of two (as we consider binary types) configurations with queues,
written as
({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\mid({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})

or
{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
.
The semantics of systems is defined by an LTS over the labels in
Equation

2.3
and the transition rules
in
Equation

2.6
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c @{\quad} c@{\quad} c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}}{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\quad{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}}{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\quad{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}}{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,{{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}}{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\quad{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\end{array}

(2.6)

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{com}}\text{-}{\mathtt{l}}}\right]
handles

{\mathbf{S}}_{1}
asynchronously sending
{m}
to the queue of
{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
;
rule
\left[{\mathtt{{com}}\text{-}{\mathtt{r}}}\right]
is symmetric.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{par}}\text{-}{\mathtt{l}}}\right]
allows
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}
to process the message at the head of
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}
via
\left[{\mathtt{{recv}}}\right]
;
rule
\left[{\mathtt{{par}}\text{-}{\mathtt{r}}}\right]
is symmetric.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{wait}}}\right]
time passes consistently across systems.

[Unsafe Mixed-choice]
The use of mixed-choice in asynchronous communications may result in infeasible protocols or, more concretely, systems (or types) that get stuck.
A mixed-choice is considered
unsafe
if actions of different directions compete to be performed (i.e., they are both viable at the same point in time).
Consider the system
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\mid({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})
, where
{S}_{1}
and
{S}_{2}
are
dual
and defined as follows:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c p{4ex} c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}{S}_{1}&=&\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x<5},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({x=0},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}_{1}}}\end{array}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}{S}_{2}&=&\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({y<5},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({y=0},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}_{2}}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

(2.7)

In the system
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
,
it is possible for both
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!b}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}

and
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!a}}}$}}\,{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}
to occur at the same time.
The resulting system
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ a})\mid({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ \mathtt{end},}{\penalty\ b})

is unable to receive either of message
a
or
b
,
and
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}
may be stuck waiting for interactions
from
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}
indefinitely.
Even though a deadlock can be avoided if message
a
is a delegation and
{S}^{\prime}_{1}
prescribes receiving
a
, since message
b
is never received by
{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
, then
Equation

2.7
still prescribes unsafe behaviour.

\triangle

2.3.
Duality, Well-formedness, and Progress (of TOAST)

In the untimed scenario, the composition of a well-formed binary type with its dual characterises a
protocol
, which specifies the “correct” set of interactions between a party and its co-party.
The dual of a type,
formally defined below,
is obtained by swapping the directions (
!
or
?
) of each interaction:
[Type Duality]

Given a type
{S}
, we define its
dual
type
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
as follows:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c @{\qquad} c @{\qquad} c @{\qquad} c @{\qquad} c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{\mathtt{end}}{\mathstrut}}=\mathtt{end}\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{\alpha}{\mathstrut}}=\alpha\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{\mu\alpha.{S}}{\mathstrut}}=\mu\alpha.\overline{{S}\mathstrut}\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{?}{\mathstrut}}=!\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{!}{\mathstrut}}=?\end{array}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\overline{{\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}}{\mathstrut}}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\overline{{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{\mathstrut}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}{\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle}{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{\overline{{{S}}{\mathstrut}}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\end{array}\end{array}

Notice that the time constraints on actions are identical for a type and its dual.
For example,
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {!\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x=3},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}}})\mid({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x=3},{\varnothing}\right).{\overline{{S}^{\prime}\mathstrut}}})
is clearly a system with dual types and identical clock valuations.
It is crucial to remember that both the clock constraints and valuations of each configuration are on their own local set of clocks, and that ‘
x
’ is not a clock shared by each. For this reason, in our examples we often use different clock names for dual types.
E.g.,
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {!\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x=3},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}}})\mid({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({y=3},{\varnothing}\right).{\overline{{S}^{\prime}\mathstrut}}})
.
This is purely a matter of presentation.

Later, in
1
we shall discuss
the reason for our
‘simple’ definition of duality
in regard to recursive types, as opposed to
[
BP12
,
LM16
]
.

2.3.1.
Well-formedness

We require a notion of
well-formedness
so that we may later provide guarantees of progress for types with mixed-choice.
The
formation rules for types are given in
Equation

2.8
.
Again, since we extend
[
BMVY19
]
any rules that differ
are
clearly
highlighted
.
Types are evaluated against judgements of the form:
{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}
where

{A}
is an environment containing recursive variables
and
{\delta}
is a constraint over all clocks characterising the times in which state
{S}
can be reached.
Formally
{A}::=\varnothing\mid\alpha:{\delta},{A}
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\hbox to511.26pt{\vbox to38.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 4.53 L 0 48.77 C 0 51.27 2.03 53.3 4.53 53.3 L 702.9 53.3 C 705.4 53.3 707.42 51.27 707.42 48.77 L 707.42 4.53 C 707.42 2.03 705.4 0 702.9 0 L 4.53 0 C 2.03 0 0 2.03 0 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.57 4.53 L 1.57 48.77 C 1.57 50.4 2.9 51.72 4.53 51.72 L 702.9 51.72 C 704.53 51.72 705.85 50.4 705.85 48.77 L 705.85 4.53 C 705.85 2.9 704.53 1.57 702.9 1.57 L 4.53 1.57 C 2.9 1.57 1.57 2.9 1.57 4.53 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1.0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.82863pt}{16.75821pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{72}\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\\
\\[-4.30554pt]
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{A};\penalty\ \MathOpTrue\Entails\mathtt{end}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{A};\penalty\ {\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}{A},{\alpha:{\delta}};\penalty\ {\delta}\Entails{S}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{A},{\alpha:{\delta}};\penalty\ {\delta}\Entails\alpha\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

(2.8)

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
checks well-formedness of choices with three conditions:
the first and third conditions are from the branching and delegation rules in
[
BMVY19
]
, respectively,
and
the second condition is new and critical to ensure progress of mixed-choice,
By the first condition
(feasibility)
a choice is well-formed with respect to the weakest past among all options (
\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
) given that each continuation
{S}_{i}
is well-formed
with respect to an environment
\gamma_{i}
, which models the corresponding guard updated with resets
\lambda_{i}
.
By abuse of notation we write

\delta[\lambda\mapsto 0]\models\gamma

to say
\forall{\nu}:({\nu}\models\delta[\lambda\mapsto 0])\implies({\nu}\models\gamma)
, where (
\implies
) is an implication.
This ensures that in every choice there is at least one viable action and, for example, would rule out
Section

2.1
.
The second condition
(mixed-choice)
requires all actions that can occur at the same time to have the same (send/receive) direction.
This condition allows for types modelling timeouts
(discussed later in
Section

2.3.3
)
and rules out scenarios as the one in
Section

2.2.3
.
The third condition
(delegation)
checks for well-formedness of each delegated session with respect to their corresponding initialisation constraint
{\delta}^{\prime}
.
Similar to the
(feasibility)
premise, by abuse of notation we write
\delta^{\prime}\models\gamma^{\prime}
to say
\forall{\nu}:({\nu}\models\delta^{\prime})\implies({\nu}\models\gamma^{\prime})
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
ensures termination types are
always
well-formed.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
associates variable
\alpha
with
invariant
\delta
in
A
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
ensures recursive calls are defined.

[Well-formed Configurations]

Given
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
,
{S}
is
well-formed
against
{\nu}
if,

\exists{\delta}
such that
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}

and
{\nu}\models{\delta}
.
A type
{S}
is
well-formed
if it is
well-formed
against
{\nu}_{0}
.

The rules in
Equation

2.8
check that in every reachable state (which includes every possible clock valuation) it is possible to perform the next action immediately or at some point in the future, unless the state is final.
(This is formalised as the progress property in
Section

2.3.2
.)
By these rules,
the type in
Sections

2.1
and
2.2.3

would not be well-formed.

Remark 1

(Delegation of Higher-Order TOAST)
.

Recall
Equation

2.2
in
Section

2.1
,
where higher-order types for session delegation are defined as
(\delta,S)
.
By condition
(\text{delegation})
in the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]

in
Equation

2.8
,
any interaction with a delegation payload
T=(\delta^{\prime},S^{\prime})
,
delegation type
S^{\prime}
must be
well-formed

by
\varnothing;\gamma^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
where environment
\gamma^{\prime}
corresponds to
\delta^{\prime}
and the environment for recursive variables
is empty.
In short, a

well-formed

TOAST cannot
delegate a recursive variable (
\alpha
).
Furthermore,
higher-order types
must be

flattened

(i.e., finitely represented) and
TOAST is unable to express
self-referential recursive higher-order types
(e.g.,

S^{\prime}=\mu\alpha.!l\left\langle(\delta^{\prime},S^{\prime})\right\rangle(\delta,\varnothing).\alpha
).
This contrasts
with
[
BH16
,
BP12
]
,
which allow
recursive variables to be delegated
directly,
and
[
LM16
]

which
discusses self-referential recursive higher-order types.
We feel this
is
orthogonal to the
primary
focus of this work.

2.3.2.
Progress of Types

Well-formedness, together with the
persistency and receive urgency featured in rule
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
of the semantics in
Equation

2.5

ensures that the composition of a well-formed type
{S}
with its dual
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
enjoys progress.
A system enjoys progress if its configurations with queues can continue communicating until reaching the end of the protocol; formally:
[Type Progress]

A configuration with queues
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})
is
final
if
{S}\UTU\mathtt{end}
and
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}=\varnothing
.
A system
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}

satisfies progress
if,
for all
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}_{2}
reachable from
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mid{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
:

(1)

either
({{\nu}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{1}})
and
({{\nu}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{2}})
are
final
;

(2)

or, there exists a
{t}\in\MathOpSetRealZ
such that
({{\nu}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{1}})\mid({{\nu}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{2}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}\penalty\ {\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}}$}}\,
.

We give the definition of (
\UTU
) in
Section

A.1
.
We write
{S}\UTU\mathtt{end}
if
{S}
is equivalent to
\mathtt{end}
, up-to the unfolding of recursive types. (e.g.,
\mu\alpha_{1}\dots\mu\alpha_{i}.\mathtt{end}\UTU\mathtt{end}
.)

Theorem 2

(Progress of Systems)
.

If
{S}
is
well-formed
against
{\nu}_{0}
,
then:

({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\mid({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ \overline{{S}\mathstrut},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})

satisfies progress
.

The main result of this section is that for a system composed of a
well-formed

{S}
and its dual

({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\mid({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ \overline{{S}\mathstrut}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})
, any state reached is either
final
, or allows for further
communication;
i.e., the system
satisfies progress
.
Progress is critical to ensure, via type-checking, that a protocol implementation does not reach deadlock and is free from communication mismatches.
(We introduce such a system for type-checking using TOAST
in
Section

5
.)

The main differences from
[
BMVY19
]
are not in the formulation of the theory
(e.g.,
Section

2.3.2
and the statement of
2
are basically unchanged)
but in proving
rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
is sufficient to ensure progress of asynchronous mixed-choice.
Additionally, the proof of progress
in
[
BMVY19
]

relies on a notion of persistency to a participant does not reach a point where there are no viable actions, and does so by requiring time-steps do not pass beyond the participants latest-enabled receiving action.
Due to mixed-choice, it is necessary to reformulate (and relax) this condition in the semantic rule

\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
in
Equation

2.5

to require only the latest-enabled action is not missed.
(See
Section

2.3.3
for a discussion).

2.3.3.
Compatibility

The proof of
2
is in
Appendix

A
,
and proceeds by showing that
a property of systems
called
compatibility

is preserved by transitions;
given formally in
Section

2.3.3
.
As typical for binary systems, our proof of progress builds upon a notion of
duality
between participants.
Since communication is asynchronous, each party may break duality with their co-party.
Compatibility allows the duality of participants to be broken, only if, doing so does not violate
communication safety
.
More specifically, compatibility requires that any message that arrives in a message queue
is expected and therefore, able to be received
and that the resulting configurations are still
compatible
.
In practice,
compatibility allows each party to behave
independently, regardless the state of the other party, while retaining the essence of
duality
, and guaranteeing that all messages will eventually be received.

[Compatibility]

We write
({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\Compat({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})

iff
({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})
and
({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})
are
compatible
.
Given
({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})
and
({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})
,
we define
compatibility
as the largest relation satisfying each of the following conditions:

(1)

(\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\varnothing)\text{\ or\ }(\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}=\varnothing)

(2)

(\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}={m};\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{1})\implies(({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{1}})\text{\ and\ }({{\nu}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{1}})\Compat({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}))

(3)

(\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}={m};\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{2})\implies(({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{2}})\text{\ and\ }({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\Compat({{\nu}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}_{2}}))

(4)

(\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}=\varnothing=\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2})\implies({S}_{1}=\overline{{S}_{2}\mathstrut}\text{\ and\ }{\nu}_{1}={\nu}_{2})

Intuitively,
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
are compatible, written
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}\Compat{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
,
if:
(
1
) at most one of their queues is non-empty (equivalent to a half duplex automaton),
(
2
–
3
) a type is always able to process any message that arrives in its queue, and
(
4
) if both queues are empty then
{\mathbf{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{2}
have dual types and same clock valuations.

[Weak Persistency]
In language-based approaches to timed semantics
[
KY06
]
time actions are always possible, even if they bring the model into a stuck state by preventing available actions. Execution traces are then filtered
as necessary,
removing all ‘bad’ traces (defined on the basis of final states).
In contrast, and to facilitate the reasoning on process behaviour, we adopt a process-based approach, that only allows for actions that characterise
intended
executions of the model
(as in
[
BBM18
,
BMVY19
]
).
Precisely, we build on the semantics of
[
BBM18
]
for asynchronous timed automata with mixed-choice,
where time actions are possible only if they do not disable:

{enumerate*}
[label=(),itemjoin=,,itemjoin*=, and ]

the latest-enabled sending action

the (latest-enabled) receiving action if the queue is not empty.

This ensures that time actions preserve the viability of at least one action (
weak-persistency
).
In our scenario, constraint
2.3.3
is too strict. Consider type
{S}
and its dual
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
below:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c p{0.5ex} c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}{S}&=&\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{data}{\left\langle{\mathtt{String}}\right\rangle}\left({x<3},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{timeout}{}\left({x\geq 4},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}\end{array}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}\overline{{S}\mathstrut}&=&\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{data}{\left\langle{\mathtt{String}}\right\rangle}\left({y<3},{\varnothing}\right).{\overline{{S}^{\prime}\mathstrut}}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{timeout}{}\left({y\geq 4},{\varnothing}\right).{\mathtt{end}}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

According to
2.3.3
, it would never be possible for
{S}
to take the
timeout
branch since a time action of
{t}\geq 3
would disable the latest-enabled send (
data
).
This is reasonable in a setting where the behaviour of such a mixed-choice has no guarantee of a timeout being received later, such as in
[
BBM18
]
.
However, this is not the case in our setting as we can rely on duality
to guarantee that
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
will send
timeout
in all executions where
y\geq 4
.
Our new
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
rule – condition
(persistency)
– implements a more general constraint
than
2.3.3
,
requiring that one latest-enabled (send or receive) action is preserved.
Constraint
2.3.3
remains as condition
(urgency)
and, for instance, prevents
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
from sending
timeout
if

data

is waiting in the queue when
y<3
.
For example,
our semantics permit the following sequence of transitions:

({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}=2}}}$}}\,({{\nu}[x\mapsto 2],}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!data\langle\mathtt{String}\rangle}}}$}}\,({{\nu}[x\mapsto 2],}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})

where
data
is sent after 2 time units.
Alternatively,
by our semantics, the following holds:

({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}=4}}}$}}\,({{\nu}[x\mapsto 4],}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?timeout}}}$}}\,({{\nu}[x\mapsto 4],}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ timeout;\varnothing})\,{{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}^{\prime}}}}$}}}\,\qquad({t}^{\prime}\in\MathOpSetRealZ)

where condition
(urgency)
ensures no time can pass
since a message currently in the queue that can be received via rule
\left[{\mathtt{{recv}}}\right]
:

({{\nu}[x\mapsto 4],}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ timeout;\varnothing})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,({{\nu}[x\mapsto 4],}{\penalty\ \mathtt{end},}{\penalty\ \varnothing})
.

\triangle

3.
A Calculus for Processes with Timeouts

We present a new calculus for timed processes which extends existing
timed session calculi
[
BLY15
,
BMVY19
]
with
timeouts and time-sensitive conditional statements.
Timeouts are defined on receive actions and may be immediately followed by sending actions, hence providing an instance of mixed-choice – which is normally not supported.
Time-sensitive conditional statements
(i.e.,
\mathtt{if}\text{-}\mathtt{then}\text{-}\mathtt{else}
with conditions on process timers)
provide a natural counter-part to the timeout construct and enhance the expressiveness of the typing system in
[
BMVY19
]
.
To better align processes with TOAST, send and select actions have been streamlined by each message consisting of both a label
{l}
and some message
variable
{v}
,
which is either data or a delegated session; the same holds for receive/branch actions.

Processes are defined by the grammar given in
Equation

3.1
.
Participants are the
endpoints
of session and are denoted by
p
and
q
.
Within a (binary) session, endpoints
p
and
q
communicate over channels
pq
and
qp
, where
p
sends to
q
over
pq
, and
q
sends to
p
over
qp
.
We
also
use

a
and
b

as ad-hoc roles,
with
channels

ab
and
ba
,
when discussing multiple binary sessions.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l @{\qquad} l@{\ }c@{\ }l @{\quad} l @{}}{P},{Q}&::=&\mathtt{set}\,\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to9.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.75371pt\lower-4.75371pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

(3.1)

Processes are equipped with timers that can be set and reset during the process execution.
Let
\MathOpSetOfTimers

be the set of timers
denoted by
x
,
y
and
z
.
While clocks are used in types to express the time constraints of a protocol (i.e., type), timers are used in processes to acquire awareness of relative time-passing during execution (as timer
concurrency
primitives in languages like Go and Erlang).
Process
\mathtt{set}\,\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to9.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.75371pt\lower-4.75371pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.3 0 C 6.3 3.48 3.48 6.3 0 6.3 C -3.48 6.3 -6.3 3.48 -6.3 0 C -6.3 -3.48 -3.48 -6.3 0 -6.3 C 3.48 -6.3 6.3 -3.48 6.3 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.85764pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{75}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.{{P}}
creates a
process timer

x
,
initialises it to
0
and continues as
{P}
.
If a process timer
named

x
already exists it is reset to
0
.
Note, there is no correspondence between the clocks used in the constraints of types and process timers, regardless if they have matching names/labels.

Process
p
\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{l}}\left({v}\right)\end{array}
.
{P}
is the select/send process: it selects label
{l}
and sends payload
{v}
to endpoint
{pq}
, and continues as
{P}
.
Its counterpart is the branch/receive process
p
{}^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.
It receives one of the labels
{l}_{i}
, instantiates
{v}_{i}
with the received payload, and continues as
{P}_{i}
.
Parameter
{e}
is a deadline for the receive action.
It can be either
\diamond{n}
or
\infty
.
If
{e}
is
\infty
, then the receive action will block until a message is received, waiting potentially forever.
If
{e}
is
\diamond{n}
, then
{n}
is the upper-bound of the receive action and
\diamond
specifies whether the wait duration is exclusive
(<)
or inclusive
(\leq)
.
For example, setting
{e}
to
(\leq 3)
specifies waiting
up-to and including
3 time units,
while
(<3)
specifies waiting
strictly less than
3 time units.
Setting
{e}
to
(\leq 0)
models a non-blocking receive action on branches that expect messages to be immediately available to receive.
Process
p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}

is the timeout process, an extended version of the branch/receive process, where
\diamond{n}
specifies the receive deadline, after which the timeout process
{Q}
gets triggered.
We specify timeouts to be
(\diamond{n})
to tactically ensure timeouts cannot be
\infty
, which would make
{Q}
dead code and would be equivalent to a branch/receive action without a timeout.
For simplicity,
in branch/receive and timeout processes:
(i) we omit the brackets in the case of a single option (i.e.,
\left\lvert I\right\rvert=1
)
and (ii) for options with no payloads we omit
({v})
.

Process
\mathtt{if}\penalty\ {c}\penalty\ \mathtt{then}\penalty\ {{P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{else}\penalty\ {{Q}}
is a conditional statement.
The condition
{c}
is a constraint on process timers, and is notably simpler than those constraints found
in
Equation

2.1
for types.
Process
\mathtt{delay}\left({{d}}\right).{{P}}
models time passing
where
{d}
models a non-deterministic delay.
At runtime
process
\mathtt{delay}\left({{d}}\right).{{P}}

is reduced to process
\mathtt{delay}\left({{t}^{\prime}}\right).{{P}}
,
for some

{t}^{\prime}\models{d}\left[{\nicefrac{{{t}^{\prime}}}{{{t}}}}\right]
,
where the non-deterministic duration
{d}
is resolved into a single duration
{t}^{\prime}
.

Recursive processes are defined by a process variable
X
and
parameters
\vec{v}
and
\vec{r}
, containing
base type
values and session channels, respectively.
As standard
[
BCD+08
,
BYY14
,
HYC08
,
Vas12
,
YV07
]
the process calculus allows parallel processes
{P}\mid{Q}
and scoped processes
({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}}
between endpoints
p
and
q
.
The end process is
0
.
Endpoints communicate over pairs of channels
{pq}
and
{qp}
, each with their own unbounded FIFO buffers
{h}
.

3.1.
Well-formed Processes

We assume that sessions within a process have already been instantiated, meaning that rather than relying on reduction rules to produce correct session instantiation, we rely on a syntactic well-formedness assumption, as in
[
BMVY19
]
.
A
well-formed process

P

consists of sessions of the form
({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {\bigl({P}^{\prime}\mid{Q}\mid{{qp}:{h}}\mid{{pq}:{h}^{\prime}}\bigr)}
and can be checked syntactically
by function
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}}\right)

given in
Section

3.1
.
[Well-formed Process]

The function
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}}\right)
is defined inductively as:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}}\right)=\begin{cases}\MathOpTrue&\text{if\ }{P}\in\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\texttt{0},\penalty\ X\langle{\vec{v};\vec{r}}\rangle,\penalty\ {{qp}:{h}}\end{array}\\[4.73611pt]
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\land\left(\mathtt{fq}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)=\{{pq},{qp}\}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}=({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}^{\prime}}\\[3.01385pt]
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\land\left(\mathtt{fq}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)=\varnothing\right)&\text{if\ }{P}\in\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{l}}\left({v}\right)\end{array}.{{P}^{\prime}},\penalty\ \mathtt{set}\,\hbox to8.86pt{\vbox to8.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-4.43022pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.85 0 C 5.85 3.23 3.23 5.85 0 5.85 C -3.23 5.85 -5.85 3.23 -5.85 0 C -5.85 -3.23 -3.23 -5.85 0 -5.85 C 3.23 -5.85 5.85 -3.23 5.85 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.62152pt}{-1.93748pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{75}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.{P}^{\prime},\\
\mathtt{delay}\left({{d}}\right).{P}^{\prime},\penalty\ \mathtt{delay}\left({{t}}\right).{P}^{\prime}\end{array}\\[4.73611pt]
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\land\mathtt{wf}\left({{Q}}\right)&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if\ }{P}\in\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\penalty\ \mathtt{if}\penalty\ {c}\penalty\ \mathtt{then}\penalty\ {{P}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \mathtt{else}\penalty\ {{Q}},\\
\penalty\ \mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{{Q}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[4.73611pt]
\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\land\mathtt{wf}\left({{Q}}\right)\land\left(\mathtt{ft}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\cap\mathtt{ft}\left({{Q}}\right)=\varnothing\right)&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if\ }{P}={{P}^{\prime}\mid{Q}}\end{array}\\[4.73611pt]
\bigwedge_{i\in I}\mathtt{wf}\left({{P}_{i}}\right)\land\left(\mathtt{fq}\left({{P}_{i}}\right)=\varnothing\right)&\text{if\ }{P}=p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\\[4.73611pt]
\mathtt{wf}\left({p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}}\right)\land\mathtt{wf}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}=p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}\end{cases}\end{array}

where
function
\mathtt{fq}\left({{P}}\right)
returns the set of queues present in process
P
that are
not
contained within the respective scope of their corresponding session,
and function
\mathtt{ft}\left({{P}}\right)
returns the set of process timers used in process
P
.
(We relegate the definitions of
\mathtt{fq}\left({{P}}\right)
and
\mathtt{ft}\left({{P}}\right)

to the appendix,
see
Sections

B.1
and
B.1
.)
By
Section

3.1
,
a
well-formed
process must:
(a) not contain any
free queues
other than those belonging to ongoing (binary) sessions contained within
scoped processes

({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}}
, which should each contain the two queues necessary for participants to exchange messages,
and (b) not contain any parallel processes that make use of the
same
process timer, i.e., process timers must only be used by a single process.

Hereafter, we assume that a process is
well-formed
, unless stated otherwise.

3.2.
Process Reduction

The semantics of processes are given in
Figure

2
, as a reduction relation on pairs of the form
({{{\theta}},{{P}}})
i.e., a processes
P
with an timer environment
{\theta}
that maps timers to their values in the current state (formally given
below
in
Section

3.2
).

[Timer Environment]

Recall
\MathOpSetOfTimers
is the
set of timers,
ranged over by
x
,
y
and
z
.
A
timer environment

{\theta}
is a linear map
{\theta}:\MathOpSetOfTimers\mapsto\MathOpSetRealZ
,
from timers to
valuations, defined:

\text{dom}({{\theta}_{1}})\cap\text{dom}({{\theta}_{2}})=\varnothing
.

(3.5)

Figure 2
.
Rules of process reduction

3.2.1.
Reduction Rules

The reduction relation is defined on two kinds of reduction: instantaneous communication actions (
\,\rightharpoonup^{\mbox{}}\,
), and time-consuming actions (
\,\rightsquigarrow^{\mbox{}}\,
). We write
\,\longrightarrow^{\mbox{}}\,
to denote a reduction that is either by (
\,\rightharpoonup^{\mbox{}}\,
) or (
\,\rightsquigarrow^{\mbox{}}\,
).

Rules
\left[{\mathtt{{Str}}}\right]
,
\left[{\mathtt{{Scope}}}\right]
,
\left[{\mathtt{{Par}}\text{-}{\mathtt{L}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{Par}}\text{-}{\mathtt{R}}}\right]
are as standard
[
BCD+08
,
HYC08
,
Vas12
,
YV07
]
.
The rule for structural congruence
\left[{\mathtt{{Str}}}\right]
is the only rule that applies to both instantaneous and time-consuming actions.
Structural equivalence follows as standard
[
HYC08
,
Mil99
,
Pie02
,
Vas12
,
YV07
]
,
with additions from
[
BYY14
,
BMVY19
]
.
Formally given below:
[Structural Congruence]

We define

{P}\equiv{Q}

as
the smallest relation on processes:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}{{{P}\mid\texttt{0}}\equiv{{P}}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{(P\mid Q)}\equiv{(Q\mid P)}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{(P_{1}\mid P_{2})\mid Q}\equiv{(P_{1}\mid Q)\mid P_{2}}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{P\mid Q}\equiv{Q\mid P}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {\texttt{0}}}\equiv{\texttt{0}}}\\[-5.16666pt]
\\
{{\mathtt{delay}\left({0}\right).{P}}\equiv{{P}}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {({\nu}{ab})\penalty\ {{P}}}}\equiv{({\nu}{ab})\penalty\ {({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}}}}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}\mid{Q}}}\equiv{({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {({P}\mid{Q})}}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{if\ }{pq}\not\in\mathtt{fn}\left({{Q}}\right)}\\[-5.16666pt]
\\
{{({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{P}}}\equiv{({\nu}{{qp}})\penalty\ {{P}}}}\hskip 17.00024pt{{(\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{{P}^{\prime}})\mid{Q}}\equiv{\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{({P}^{\prime}\mid{Q})}}\hskip 8.50012pt\text{if\ }X\langle{\vec{v^{\prime}};\vec{r^{\prime}}}\rangle\not\in\mathtt{fpv}\left({{Q}}\right)}\end{array}

where

\mathtt{fn}\left({{Q}}\right)
and
\mathtt{fpv}\left({{Q}}\right)
return the set of free names and process variables in
{Q}
, respectively.

3.2.2.
Communication Rules,
Equation

3.3

Rules
\left[{\mathtt{{Send}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}}\right]
are standard
[
Mil99
]
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Send}}}\right]
a process inserts a message
{{l}}{{v}}
into the queue of the other party,
while by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}}\right]
a process may remove an expected message from their queue and continue on the corresponding branch.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}\text{-}{\mathtt{T}}}\right]
is similar to rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}}\right]
except, as we will discuss shortly
in
Section

3.3
,
it allows the timeout branch
Q
to be taken if the process does not receive a message within duration
{e}
.
(We include rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}\text{-}{\mathtt{T}}}\right]
for consistency, but hereafter only refer to rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}}\right]
.)

3.2.3.
Recursion Rules,
Equation

3.4

Both rules
\left[{\mathtt{{Def}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{Call}}}\right]
are unchanged from
[
BMVY19
]
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Def}}}\right]
defines a recursive process
P

and allows the recursive body
Q
to reduce.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Call}}}\right]
handles recursive calls of predefined recursive processes and yields a process configuration consisting of the next iteration of the recursive process using the parameters provided by the process variable
X
;
the parallel process
Q
is unaffected.

3.2.4.
Time-sensitive Rules,
Equation

3.5

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Reset}}}\right]
resets a process timer
x
in the timer environment
{\theta}
to 0.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{If}}\text{-}{\mathtt{T}}}\right]
selects branch
{P}
if time-sensitive condition
{c}
holds for
{\theta}
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{If}}\text{-}{\mathtt{F}}}\right]
is symmetric, selecting
{Q}
if
{c}
does not hold for
{\theta}
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Det}}}\right]

determines the exact duration
{t}^{\prime}
of a non-deterministic runtime delay modelled by
{d}
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Delay}}}\right]
handles delay processes, so long as they are defined in
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
which,
in short, returns process
{P}
after
{t}
units of time have elapsed.
(We formally define
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
below in
Section

3.3
.)
Additionally, by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Delay}}}\right]
any delay on a process also causes the timers within
{\theta}
to elapse accordingly.

3.3.
Time Passing

The definition of
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
is given in
Section

3.3
.
The first two cases in
Section

3.3
model the effect of time passing on branching and timeout processes.
The third case is for time-consuming processes.
The fourth case distributes time passing in parallel compositions and ensures that time passes for all parts of the system equally.
The remaining cases define the processes where time is allowed to pass.
[Time Passing Function]

The time-passing function
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
is a partial function only defined for the cases below:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\Phi_{t}\left({p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}}\right)=\begin{cases}p^{\diamond{n}-{t}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}&\text{if\ }({{t}\diamond{n}})\\[2.41109pt]
\Phi_{{t}-{n}}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\[15.6491pt]
\Phi_{t}\left({p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}}\right)=\begin{cases}p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}&\text{if\ }{{e}=\infty}\\[4.13332pt]
p^{{n}-{t}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}&\text{if\ }{{e}=\diamond{n}}\text{\ and\ }({t}\diamond{n})\end{cases}\\[15.6491pt]
\Phi_{t}\left({\mathtt{delay}\left({{t}^{\prime}}\right).{P}}\right)=\begin{cases}\mathtt{delay}\left({{t}^{\prime}-{t}}\right).{P}&\text{if\ }{t}^{\prime}\geq{t}\\[0.0pt]
\Phi_{{t}-{t}^{\prime}}\left({{P}}\right)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\[15.6491pt]
\Phi_{t}\left({pq:{h}}\right)=pq:{h}\hskip 17.00024pt\Phi_{t}\left({{P}_{1}\mid{P}_{2}}\right)=\Phi_{t}\left({{P}_{1}}\right)\mid\Phi_{t}\left({{P}_{2}}\right)\hskip 8.50012pt\text{if\ }(\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}_{i}}\right)\cap\mathtt{NEQ}\left({P_{j}}\right)=\varnothing)\text{\ and\ }i\neq j\in\left\{1,2\right\}\\[8.5359pt]
\Phi_{t}\left({\texttt{0}}\right)=\texttt{0}\hskip 17.00024pt\Phi_{t}\left({({\nu}{pq})\penalty\ {{P}}}\right)=({\nu}{pq})\penalty\ {\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)}\hskip 17.00024pt\Phi_{t}\left({\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{{Q}}}\right)=\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{\Phi_{t}\left({{Q}}\right)}\end{array}

The auxiliary functions
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}}\right)
and
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{P}}\right)
are given in
Figure

3
.
Together, they indicate which role (if any) is able to receive,
and are used in
Section

3.3
to ensure

receive urgency
,
by requiring that there are no processes that are both waiting to receive and have a non-empty queue.
Informally,
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}}\right)
returns the set of channels on which
{P}
is waiting to receive a message,
and
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{P}}\right)
returns the set of endpoints with a non-empty inbound queue.

[Time Passing Function]
In
Section

3.3

we define
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
as a
partial function

in order to:
(a) ensure that certain processes are always non-blocking and can never be arbitrarily delayed (e.g., send processes)
and (b) make it easier to differentiate between a process that can not be delayed, and a process that can be delayed for 0.
For example, consider the process:

P=\mathtt{delay}(5).P^{\prime}\mid p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{data}\end{array}.{P^{\prime\prime}}
.
Under the current definition of
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
, it is clear that the
data
must be sent prior to any delay occurring, and in this instance, the definition of
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)

in
Section

3.3

enforces that certain processes, such as send processes, should never be delayed under any circumstances.
(The only exception being a formally defined delay process prefixed to a send process.)
However,
if
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
were to be defined for all cases of
P

including send processes,
the reduction of our example process
P
becomes unclear and may be indefinitely reduced via rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Delay}}}\right]
for a delay of 0.
Furthermore, by the current definition of
structural congruence in
Section

3.2.1
,
sending
data
may be skipped altogether.
Therefore, we tactically define
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
to be only a partial function in order to distinctly group delayable and non-delayable processes.
Consider the process below:

{P}=({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {\bigl(p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}\mid{qp}:\varnothing\mid{Q}^{\prime}\bigr)}

For a time-consuming action of
{t}
to occur on
{P}
it is required that
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
is defined for all parallel components in
{P}
.
Suppose
{t}={n}+1
so that we can observe the expiring of the timeout. The evaluation of
\Phi_{t}\left({p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}}}\right)
results in the evaluation of
\Phi_{1}\left({{Q}}\right)
.
If
{Q}
were e.g., a sending process, then
\Phi_{{t}^{\prime}}\left({{Q}}\right)
is not defined for any
{t}^{\prime}>0
.
Otherwise, if
\Phi_{1}\left({{Q}}\right)
and
\Phi_{t}\left({{Q}^{\prime}}\right)
are defined,
then
{t}
may pass and

\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)=({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {\bigl(\Phi_{1}\left({{Q}}\right)\mid{qp}:\varnothing\mid\Phi_{t}\left({{Q}^{\prime}}\right)\bigr)}
.

\triangle

[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}}\right)&=&\begin{cases}\{p\}&\text{if\ }{P}\in\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}p^{\diamond{n}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {{Q}},\penalty\ p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\end{array}\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{Q}}\right)\setminus\{p,q\}&\text{if\ }{P}=({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{Q}}\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}=\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{{Q}}\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\cup\mathtt{Wait}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}={P}^{\prime}\mid{Q}\\[1.72221pt]
\varnothing&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\\
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{P}}\right)&=&\begin{cases}\{p\}&\text{if\ }{P}={qp}:{h}\land{h}\neq\varnothing\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{Q}}\right)\setminus\{p,q\}&\text{if\ }{P}=({\nu}{{pq}})\penalty\ {{Q}}\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}=\mathtt{def\ }{X({\vec{v};\vec{r}})={P}^{\prime}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{{Q}}\\[1.72221pt]
\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{P}^{\prime}}\right)\cup\mathtt{NEQ}\left({{Q}}\right)&\text{if\ }{P}={P}^{\prime}\mid{Q}\\[1.72221pt]
\varnothing&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}\end{array}

.
Definition of
\mathtt{Wait}\left({{P}}\right)
and

.

4.
Expressiveness

In this section we reflect on the expressiveness of our mixed-choice extension, particularly in regard to
[
BMVY19
]
, using examples to illustrate differences.
Given the increase in expressiveness, we discuss how type-checking becomes more interesting with the inclusion of

\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
and give an intuition on the relationship between the TOAST and processes.

4.1.
Missing deadlines

The process corresponding to
{?\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({\MathOpTrue},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}}

is merely
p^{\infty}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{a}{}:{P}\end{array}
, which waits to receive
a
forever.
By way of contrast,
{?\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({x<3},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}}^{\prime}
, cannot receive when
x\geq 3
, requiring the process to take the form:

p^{<3}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{b}{}:{P}^{\prime}\end{array}
.
More generally,
if we consider:

\{?b\penalty\ (x<3,\penalty\ \varnothing).S^{\prime},\penalty\ ?c\penalty\ (3<x<5,\penalty\ \varnothing).{S}^{\prime\prime}\}

when
(x\geq 3)

then, amending the previous process,
p^{<3}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{b}{}:{P}^{\prime}\end{array}\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {p^{<2}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{c}{}:{P}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}}
.

4.2.
Ping-pong protocol

The example in this section illustrates the usefulness of time-sensitive conditional statements. The
ping
-
pong
protocol consists of two participants exchanging messages between themselves on receipt of a message from the other
[
LNY22
]
.
One interpretation of the protocol is the following:

\mu\alpha.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{!ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\})}.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\\
?pong(x>3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
{!pong(x>3,\penalty\ \{x\})}.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\\
?pong(x>3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\end{array}\end{array}

where each participant exchanges the role of sender, either sending
ping
early, or
pong
late.
Without time-sensitive conditional statements, the setting in
[
BMVY19
]
only allows implementations where the choice between the ‘
ping
’ and the ‘
pong
’ branch are hard-coded.
In presence of non-deterministic delays (e.g.,
\mathtt{delay}\left({t<6}\right)
), the hard-coded choice can only be for the latest branch to ‘expire’, and the highlighted fragment of the
ping
-
pong
protocol above could be naively implemented as follows (omitting
{Q}
for simplicity):

\mathtt{def\ }{X({p})={\color[rgb]{0.58,0,0.82}{\left(\right.}}\mathtt{delay}\left({t<6}\right).p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{pong}\end{array}.{{Q}}{\color[rgb]{0.58,0,0.82}{\left.\right)}}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{X\langle{r}\rangle}

The choice of sending
ping
is
always
discarded as it may be unsatisfied in
some
executions.
The calculus in this paper, thanks to the time-awareness deriving from a process timer
y
, allows us to
potentially
honour each branch, as follows:

\mathtt{def\ }{X({p})={\color[rgb]{0.58,0,0.82}{\left(\right.}}\mathtt{set}\,\hbox to10.55pt{\vbox to10.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.27348pt\lower-5.27348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.6308pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{75}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.\mathtt{delay}\left({t<6}\right).\mathtt{if}\left({\hbox to10.55pt{\vbox to10.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.27348pt\lower-5.27348pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
