4.3.
Mixed-choice Ping-pong protocol

An alternative interpretation of the
ping
-
pong
protocol can result in an implementation with mixed-choice, as shown below:

\mu\alpha.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}!pong(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\\
?timeout(x>3,\penalty\ \varnothing).end\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
!pong(x>3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\alpha\\
!timeout(x>3,\penalty\ \varnothing).end\end{array}\\
\end{array}

where ‘
pings
’ are responded by ‘
pongs
’ and vice versa.
Notice that if a timely
ping
is not received, a
pong
is sent instead, which if not responded to by a
ping
, triggers a timeout.
Similarly, once a
ping
has been received, a
pong
must be sent on time to avoid a timeout.
Such a convoluted protocol can be fully implemented:

\mathtt{def\ }{X({p})={P}}\penalty\ \mathtt{in\ }{X\langle{r}\rangle}
where
{P}
:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{P}=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}p^{\leq 3}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{ping}{}:\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{set}\,\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to9.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.75371pt\lower-4.75371pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.3 0 C 6.3 3.48 3.48 6.3 0 6.3 C -3.48 6.3 -6.3 3.48 -6.3 0 C -6.3 -3.48 -3.48 -6.3 0 -6.3 C 3.48 -6.3 6.3 -3.48 6.3 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.85764pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{75}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.\mathtt{delay}\left({t\leq 4}\right).\mathtt{if}\left({\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to9.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.75371pt\lower-4.75371pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

4.4.
Message throttling

A real-world application of the previous example is
message throttling
.
The rationale behind message throttling is to cull unresponsive processes, which do not keep up with the message processing tempo set by the system.
This avoids a server from becoming overwhelmed by a flood of incoming messages.
In such a protocol, upon receiving a message, a participant is permitted a grace period to respond before receiving another.
The grace period is specified as a number of unacknowledged messages, rather than a period of time.
Below we present a fully parametric implementation of this behaviour, where
m
is the maximum number of
unacknowledged messages before a timeout is issued.

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\quad}l@{}}S_{0}&=&\mu\alpha^{0}.!msg(x\geq 3,\{x\}).S_{1}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
S_{i}&=&\mu\alpha^{i}.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ack(x<3,\{x\}).\alpha^{i-1},\penalty\ !msg(x\geq 3,\{x\}).S_{i+1}\end{array}\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
S_{m}&=&\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ack(x<3,\{x\}).\alpha^{m-1},\penalty\ !tout(x\geq 3,\penalty\ \varnothing).\mathtt{end}\end{array}\end{array}

(4.1)

where
0<i<m
. Below is the corresponding processes (again,
0<i<m
):

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }l@{\ }l@{\ }l@{\ }l@{}}{P}_{0}&=&\mathtt{def\ }X_{0}({p})&=&\mathtt{delay}\left({3}\right).{p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{msg}\end{array}.{{P}_{1}}}\mathtt{\ in\ }X_{0}\langle{p}\rangle\\[-6.45831pt]
\\
{P}_{i}&=&\mathtt{def\ }X_{i}({p})&=&p^{<3}\penalty\ {\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{ack}{}:X_{i-1}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\penalty\ \mathtt{after\ }{p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{msg}\end{array}.{{P}_{i+1}}}\mathtt{\ in\ }X_{i}\langle{p}\rangle\\[-6.45831pt]
\\
{P}_{m}&=&\mathrlap{p^{<3}\penalty\ {\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{ack}{}:X_{m-1}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\penalty\ \mathtt{after\ }{p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{tout}\end{array}.{\texttt{0}}}}\end{array}

(4.2)

We
now
concretize this example with the
m=2
instance
for type
S

and process
P
:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{}}S&=&\mu\alpha^{0}.!msg(x\geq 3,\{x\}).\mu\alpha^{1}.\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ack(x<3,\{x\}).\alpha^{0},\\
!msg(x\geq 3,\{x\}).\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ack(x<3,\{x\}).\alpha^{1},\\
!tout(x\geq 3,\varnothing).\mathtt{end}\end{array}\end{array}\\[-6.45831pt]
\\
P&=&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{def\ }\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}X_{0}({p})=\mathtt{delay}\left({3}\right).p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{msg}\end{array}.\\[2.15277pt]
\mathtt{def\ }\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}X_{1}({p})=\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}p^{<3}\penalty\ {\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{ack}{}:X_{0}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\\[2.15277pt]
\mathtt{after\ }p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{msg}\end{array}.p^{<3}\penalty\ {\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{ack}{}:X_{1}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\penalty\ \mathtt{after\ }p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{tout}\end{array}.{\texttt{0}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[0.43057pt]
\mathtt{in\ }X_{1}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\\[3.22916pt]
\mathtt{in\ }X_{0}\langle{p}\rangle\end{array}\end{array}

The system shown in
Figure

4

also illustrates the instance of
m=2
.

S_{1}

\mathtt{end}

5.
Type Checking TOAST Processes

Processes are validated against types using judgements of the form:

{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{P}\TypedBy{\Delta}
.
[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\ }c@{\ }l@{\quad}l@{}}{\Gamma}&::=&\varnothing\mid{{\Gamma},\penalty\ {v}:{T}}\mid{{\Gamma},\penalty\ {X\!:({\vec{T};\boldsymbol{\theta};\boldsymbol{\Delta}})}}&(\text{where ${T}$\ is a \emph{base-type}})\\[4.73611pt]

(5.1)

Variable Environments

{\Gamma}
map values
{v}
to data types
{T}
and process variables
X
to triples
{X\!:({\vec{T};\boldsymbol{\theta};\boldsymbol{\Delta}})}
, where

\vec{T}
is a vector of messages (consisting of labels and data types),
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
are sets of timer environments and session environments, respectively and both are possibly infinite.
With respect to usual formulations of session types,
we use the mapping
{\theta}
of timers (denoted
x
,
y
and
z
)
as a
timer environment (given in
Section

3.2
)
to correctly type check
time-sensitive conditional statements.
As usual,
Session Environments

{\Delta}
map roles
p
and
q
to configurations and session endpoints
{qp}
and
{pq}
to queues
\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}
.
Similarly to the syntax of processes
in
Section

3
,
we may
use

a
and
b

as ad-hoc roles,
along with endpoints
ab
and
ba
,
during discussions of more than one session.

5.1.
Auxiliary definitions & notation

We write
\text{dom}({{\Delta}})
for the domain of
{\Delta}
, and
{\Delta}_{1},{\Delta}_{2}
for
{\Delta}_{1}\cup{\Delta}_{2}
when
\text{dom}({{\Delta}_{1}})\cap\text{dom}({{\Delta}_{2}})=\varnothing
.
We define
{\Delta}(p)=({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
iff
p\in{\Delta}
and
\exists{\Delta}^{\prime}
such that
{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})}
,
and define

\mathtt{val}({\nu},{S})={\nu}

and

\mathtt{type}({\nu},{S})={S}
.
Given a time offset
{t}\in\MathOpSetRealZ
, we define:

•

{\Delta}+{t}=\{{(\mathtt{val}({\Delta}(p))+{t},\penalty\ \mathtt{type}({\Delta}(p)))}\mid{p\in\text{dom}({{\Delta}})}\}

•

{\theta}+{t}=\{\hbox to9.51pt{\vbox to9.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.75371pt\lower-4.75371pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

We say that
{\Delta}
is
{t}
-reading if for the duration
{t}
, there exist roles within
{\Delta}
that are able to perform a receiving action.
We say that
{\Delta}
is
not

{t}
-reading if there are no roles within
{\Delta}
able to receive for the duration of
{t}
. Formally:
[
{t}
-reading
{\Delta}
]

{\Delta}
is
{t}
-reading if,

\exists{t}^{\prime}<{t},p\in\text{dom}({{\Delta}})

such that
{\Delta}(p)=({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\implies({{\nu}+{t}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,
.
We write
{\Delta}
is
\diamond{t}
-reading if,

\exists{t}^{\prime}\diamond{t},p\in\text{dom}({{\Delta}})

such that
{\Delta}(p)=({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\implies({{\nu}+{t}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,
.

Section

5.1
is useful to check against violations of
receive urgency
.

2

2

See
Section

5.3
.

[Well-formed
{\Delta}
]

{\Delta}
is

for all

such that
{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})}\implies

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
by
Section

2.3.1
.

Figure 5
.
Standard & time-sensitive type checking rules

5.2.
Type Checking Rules

The
type checking
rules are given in
Figures

5
and
6
.

Figure

5
shows standard typing rules in
Equation

5.2
and time-sensitive typing rules in
Equation

5.3
.

Figure

6
shows rules pertaining to communication, with:
sending processes in
Equation

5.4
,
branching in
Equation

5.5
,
receptions in
Equation

5.6
,
and queues in
Equation

5.7
.
We will now discuss each rule.

5.2.1.
Standard Rules,
Equation

5.2

Terminated processes and empty channel buffers are typed using axioms
\left[{\mathtt{{End}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{Empty}}}\right]
, respectively.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Weak}}}\right]
handles the type-checking
of a session where an individual process has successfully reached termination, and where the rest of the session may be ongoing.
Such an instance is to be expected in the asynchronous setting, where one party may perform a sequence of non-blocking sending actions and finish, leaving the other party to process and receive.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Par}}}\right]
divides
the session environment
into two disjoint environments,

{\Delta}_{1}
and
{\Delta}_{2}
,
so that each session is carried by only one of
{P}
and
{Q}
.
The timer environment is also split into two disjoint environments,
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
,
as processes would otherwise be capable of indirectly communicating or influencing the behaviour of other processes
outside the interactions prescribed by the types.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Res}}}\right]
ensures scoped processes are well-typed against binary sessions composed of
compatible
configurations (by
Section

2.3.3
).
Notably, the only change from
[
BMVY19
]
in this rule is the well-formedness requirement of
{S}_{i}
against
\nu_{i}
(see
Section

2.3.1
). In
[
BMVY19
]
, well-formedness was naturally entailed by the premises of their
rules type-checking sending/receiving processes.
In order to tackle the complexity of checking mixed-choice (branching and selections), we have used multiple simpler rules that make it difficult to encapsulate such a requirement. Therefore, without loss of expressiveness, we have made
the
requirement
explicit here.

Rules
\left[{\mathtt{{Rec}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{Var}}}\right]
are essentially unchanged from
[
BMVY19
]
,
only now extended with process timers.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Rec}}}\right]
handles recursion definitions by
evaluating
{Q}
and collecting within the triple
({\vec{T};\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{\boldsymbol{\theta}};\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}})
all messages, timer and session environments present at any corresponding call.
This triple
is mapped to a process variable assigned to
{P}
, and added to
\Gamma
as the evaluation continues with
{Q}
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Var}}}\right]
determines if a call is well-typed by checking that the provided messages and channels correspond to those in the process variable.

5.2.2.
Time-sensitive Rules,
Equation

5.3

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Timer}}}\right]
handles setting or resetting of timer
x
.
We require
x
to be already in
{\theta}
in the set case (also handled by this rule) to avoid race conditions on timers with concurrent sessions, which would compromise subject reduction.
Inspection of the syntactic structure of a process is enough to know beforehand which timers will be used.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Del}}\text{-}{{d}}}\right]
checks that
{P}
is well-typed
against all solutions of
{d}
.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Del}}\text{-}{{t}}}\right]
reflects time passing on
{\theta}
and the session
{\Delta}
.
The right-hand side premise of ‘
\Delta\text{\ not\ }t\text{-reading}
’ enforces
receive urgency
in processes by requiring that no participants in
\Delta
are able to receive a message from their queue for the duration of
t
.
(Inverse to
Section

5.1
.)
Rules
\left[{\mathtt{{IfTrue}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{IfFalse}}}\right]
are for typing time-sensitive conditional statements.
If the timers in
{\theta}
satisfy the
condition
{c}
,
then by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{IfTrue}}}\right]
the evaluation continues with
{P}
.
Else, by the symmetric rule
\left[{\mathtt{{IfFalse}}}\right]
the evaluation continues with
{Q}
.

[]

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{l}}\left({v}\right)\end{array}.{{P}}}\TypedBy{\Delta},\penalty\ {p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})}\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{}}\exists i\in I:({l}={l}_{i})\land({\nu}\models{\delta}_{i})\land({T}_{i}\penalty\ \text{\emph{base-type}})\land({\Gamma}\Entails{v}:{T}_{i})\land\mbox{}\\[3.01385pt]
(\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}=!)\land{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{{P}}\TypedBy{\Delta},{p:({{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right],}{\penalty\ {S}_{i}})}\end{array}}\end{array}\\[-2.15277pt]
\\
\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{p\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{l}}\left(b\right)\end{array}.{{P}}}\TypedBy{\Delta},\penalty\ {p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})},\penalty\ {b:({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})}\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{}}\exists i\in I:({l}={l}_{i})\land({\nu}\models{\delta}_{i})\land({T}_{i}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}\end{array})\land({\nu}^{\prime}\models{\delta}^{\prime})\land\mbox{}\\[3.01385pt]
(\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}=!)\land{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{{P}}\TypedBy{\Delta},{p:({{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right],}{\penalty\ {S}_{i}})}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

(5.4)

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}\TypedBy{\Delta},\penalty\ {p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}}\,{{l}_{j}}\left\langle{{T}_{j}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{j}},{{\lambda}_{j}}}\right)}.{{S}_{j}}\end{array}_{j\in J}})}\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{}}\neg(\left\lvert J\right\rvert=\left\lvert I\right\rvert=1)\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\forall j\in J:({\nu}\models{\delta}_{j})\implies(\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}=?)\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \exists i\in I:({l}_{i}={l}_{j})\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mbox{}\\[3.01385pt]

(5.5)

\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}c@{}}{{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{{l}}{\left({{v}}\right)}:{P}\end{array}}\TypedBy{\Delta},\penalty\ {p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {?\mathstrut}{{l}}{\left\langle{{T}}\right\rangle}\left({\delta},{\lambda}\right).{{S}}})}\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{}}{T}\penalty\ \text{\emph{base-type}}\quad{\Delta}\penalty\ \text{not\ ${e}$-reading}\quad{\forall{t}:({\nu}+{t}\models{\delta})\MathOpBimplies({t}\in{e})}\\[3.01385pt]

(5.7)

Figure 6
.
Rules for type-checking communicating processes against TOAST

5.2.3.
Sending Rules,
Equation

5.4

Rules
\left[{\mathtt{{VSend}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{DSend}}}\right]
are for typing processes that send messages against a choice type, by finding a matching pair of labels in the process and type.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{VSend}}}\right]
applies for sending base-types, requiring that
{v}
is well-typed against
{T}
, and the continuation process
{P}
is well-typed against the continuation
{S}
, with any clock resets applied.
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{DSend}}}\right]
is similar,
but for delegating an ongoing session with role
b
to role
p
,
and then
removes role
b
from the session environment.

5.2.4.
Branching Rules,
Equation

5.5

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Branch}}}\right]
checks a branching process against a choice type
and is comprised of a single premise.
The first
condition
of the premise
requires that
the type or the processes has more than one element, to ensure that type-checking is deterministic (if both are singleton sets, then either rule
\left[{\mathtt{{DRecv}}}\right]
or
\left[{\mathtt{{VRecv}}}\right]
in
Equation

5.6
should be used).
The second
condition
of the premise
requires that each currently enabled receiving action in the type has a corresponding branch in the process (i.e., since all labels are unique, they have matching labels) which when paired together, are
well-typed
.

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Timeout}}}\right]
checks that a
\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
process is well-typed against a choice type; decomposing the checking into two parts:
the branch process, and the process
{Q}
; requiring that both are
well-typed
.
The first line in the premise forms a judgement to be checked by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Branch}}}\right]
, by discarding the ‘timeout’ part of the original process.
The second line
of the premise
checks
the process is still
well-typed
in the case where the
timeout ‘triggers’ (i.e., time
{n}
passes).
Put simply,
each enabled action prescribed by the type must have a corresponding branch in the process that, when paired together, form a well-typed judgement.

5.2.5.
Reception Rules,
Equation

5.6

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{VRecv}}}\right]
is applied when evaluating individual base-type receptions.
The first line of the premise checks that waiting the duration of
{n}
adheres to the timings specified by the type’s constraints
{\delta}
; also, this line requires that there are no other roles in
{\Delta}
that have enabled receiving actions.
The second line of the premise evaluates the proceeding process
{P}
against the continuation type
{S}
, given any possible delay that could occur (
{t}\diamond{n}
).
Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{DRecv}}}\right]
is similar, but for evaluating individual
delegation
receptions, which are then added to the session environment.

5.2.6.
Queue Rules,
Equation

5.7

Non-empty buffers and queues are typed by
\left[{\mathtt{{VQue}}}\right]
or rule
\left[{\mathtt{{DQue}}}\right]
.
The former is for
base-type
payloads and the latter for delegated sessions.

5.3.
Type Checking Examples

The handling of scope restriction, parallel composition, and recursion
is similar to existing formulations.
We give an example of the less obvious rules to handle timers and mixed-choice.
Precisely,
we show
in
Section

5.3

how to type check an implementation of a simplified (non-recursive version) the mixed choice
ping
-
pong
protocol featured
in
Section

4.3
.
Later, in
Section

5.3

we discuss type-checking multiple binary sessions, and illustrate the issues that can arise from
incompatible interleavings
.

[Type Checking Ping-pong]
Consider the protocol
S
below:

{S}=\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}!pong(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\mathtt{end},\\
?timeout(x>3,\penalty\ \varnothing).\mathtt{end}\end{array},\\[-4.30554pt]
\\
!pong(x>3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}?ping(x\leq 3,\penalty\ \{x\}).\mathtt{end},\\
!timeout(x>3,\penalty\ \varnothing).\mathtt{end}\end{array}\\
\end{array}

and its candidate implementations

).

The case for the ‘

’ process in
