C
.

\triangle

[Incompatible Interleavings]
Consider the following session environments:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}lcl@{}}{\Delta}_{ab}&=&{a:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})},\penalty\ {b:({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})},\penalty\ {ba:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}},\penalty\ {ab:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}\\[2.15277pt]
{\Delta}_{pq}&=&{p:({{\nu}_{3},}{\penalty\ {S}_{3}})},\penalty\ {q:({{\nu}_{4},}{\penalty\ {S}_{4}})},\penalty\ {qp:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{3}},\penalty\ {pq:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{4}}\end{array}

where both
{\Delta}_{ab}
and
{\Delta}_{pq}
are
well-formed
and composed together, under the session environment
{\Delta}=({\Delta}_{ab},{\Delta}_{pq})
.
Both
p
and
b
are roles performed by the same participant in two distinct sessions.
All clocks within
{\Delta}
are 0.
Below are the definitions of
{S}_{2}
and
{S}_{3}
:

\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{}}\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}{S}_{2}=\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{request}{\left\langle{\mathtt{String}}\right\rangle}\left({x<5},{\left\{x\right\}}\right).{{S}^{\prime}_{2}}}{\\[2.15277pt]
}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{!\mathstrut}{timeout}{}\left({x\geq 5},{\left\{x\right\}}\right).{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{2}}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}{S}_{3}=\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\rule{0.0pt}{6.45831pt}{{?\mathstrut}{message}{}\left({y>3},{\varnothing}\right).{{S}^{\prime}_{3}}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Consider the following implementation
{P}
, where role
b
first waits 5 time units for a
request
before receiving
message
as role
p
.

{P}=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}ab^{<5}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{request}{\left({\mathtt{req\_str}}\right)}:qp^{\infty}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{message}{}:{P}^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}\\[2.15277pt]
\penalty\ \mathtt{after}\penalty\ {ba\,{\MathOpPrcSend\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{timeout}\end{array}.{qp^{\infty}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{message}{}:{P}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}}}\end{array}

Assume
message
arrives in the queue
qp
at time
3.5
and
request
arrives at time
4
. Process
P
will receive
request
at time
4
while
message
is still pending in the queue, breaking
receive urgency
.
In fact,
P
is not
4
-reading since there exists a role
p
in
\Delta
that may be able to receive before
4
time units.

Section

5.1
is utilised by the
type checking
rules in
Figures

5
and
6
to ensure such implementations are
not

well-typed
.
In this case, one may observe that there exists no well-typed implementation of
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
that satisfies receive urgency, hence the roles are
fundamentally incompatible and unable to be interleaved.

\triangle

6.
Subject Reduction

In this section we establish subject reduction for our typing system, namely we establish
a relation between well-typed processes and their types that is preserved by reduction.

As usual, subject reduction is given for closed systems (
3
) with
\Gamma
and
\Delta
empty.
The proof relies on two lemmas that establish subject reduction for open systems, for time-consuming steps (
1
)
and instantaneous steps (
2
).
We give an overview in this section and relegate the full proofs to
Appendix

B
.
Crucially, by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Res}}}\right]
in
Figure

5

Equation

5.2
, in any typing judgement of an open system the sessions in
\Delta
are both
well-formed
and comprised of
compatible
configurations, enabling us to utilise progress of TOAST
2
.
Such sessions we say are
fully-balanced
.
The notion of balanced session, inspired by the one in
[
CDCSY17
]
is given in
Section

6
.

[Balanced
{\Delta}
]

Let
Bal
be the set containing all session environments
{\Delta}
, such that if
{\Delta}\in\text{Bal}
, then
{\Delta}
adheres to the following:

(1)

{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})},{{qp}:{{m};\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}}}\implies({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

and
{\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})},{{qp}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}}\in\text{Bal}
.

(2)

{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})},{{qp}:{\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}}},{q:({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})}\implies\exists\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\Compat({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})
.

(3)

{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})},{{qp}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}},{q:({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})},{{pq}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}\implies({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}})\Compat({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}})
.

[Fully
-
Balanced
{\Delta}
]

A
balanced

{\Delta}
is said to be
fully-balanced
if:

(1)

{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})}\implies\exists{\Delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},q,{\nu}_{2},{S}_{2},\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1},\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}:{\Delta}^{\prime}={\Delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},{{qp}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}},{q:({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})},{{pq}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}
.

(2)

{\Delta}={\Delta}^{\prime},{{qp}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{1}}\implies\exists{\Delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},{\nu}_{1},{S}_{1},{\nu}_{2},{S}_{2},\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}:{\Delta}^{\prime}={\Delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},{p:({{\nu}_{1},}{\penalty\ {S}_{1}})},{q:({{\nu}_{2},}{\penalty\ {S}_{2}})},{{pq}:\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}_{2}}
.

Theorem 1

(Time Step)
.

Let
{\Delta}
be
fully-balanced
and
well-formed
.
If
{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{P}\TypedBy{\Delta}
and
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
is defined,
then
{\Gamma};{\theta}+{t}\Entails\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)\TypedBy{\Delta}+{t}

and
{\Delta}+{t}
is
fully-balanced
and
well-formed
.

By
1
we prove that, given a
well-typed
process
{P}
, for any value of
{t}
such that
\Phi_{t}\left({{P}}\right)
is defined by
Section

3.3
,
{t}
passing evenly over the session environment preserves both
well-formedness
and
balancedness
.
(Similar to the preservation of
well-formedness
and
compatibility
for system configurations in
15
and
17
of
2
.)
Clearly, by inspection of the
type checking
rules in
Figures

5
and
6
, such processes would require several divisions of the initial session environment (and map of process timers) before each individual process could be evaluated.
Therefore, we rely on the notion of
balancedness
(
Section

6
).
Naturally, a
fully-balanced
session must also be
balanced
, and this allows us to reason on specific
balanced
components of a larger
fully-balanced
session environment.

Theorem 2

(Action Step)
.

Let
{\Delta}
be
balanced
and
well-formed
.
If
{\Gamma};{\theta}\Entails{P}\TypedBy{\Delta}

and
({{{\theta}},{{P}}})\!\!\,\rightharpoonup^{\mbox{}}\,\!\!({{{\theta}}^{\prime},{{P}}^{\prime}})

then
\exists{\Delta}^{\prime}:{\Delta}\!\!\,\longrightarrow^{*}\,\!\!{\Delta}^{\prime}

and

{\Gamma};{\theta}^{\prime}\!\Entails\!{P}^{\prime}\!\TypedBy\!{\Delta}^{\prime}

and

{\Delta}^{\prime}
is
balanced
and
well-formed
.

By
2
we prove, among other things, that any sending or receiving action performed by a
well-typed
process corresponds to an action prescribed by the type of the corresponding configuration, at the correct time.
The best illustration of this is perhaps
2
in
2
,
which shows the case of reduction via rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Recv}}}\right]
in
Figure

2

Equation

3.4
, and that the reduction preserves
well-typedness
.
We relegate the rules for session environment reduction
to
Figure

7
in
Appendix

B
, along with the full proofs of
1
and
2
.

Corollary 3

(Subject Reduction)
.

If
\varnothing;{\theta}\Entails\!{P}\TypedBy\varnothing

and
({{{\theta}},{{P}}})\!\rightarrow\!({{{\theta}}^{\prime},{{P}}^{\prime}})

then
\varnothing;{\theta}^{\prime}\!\Entails\!{P}^{\prime}\!\TypedBy\varnothing
.

[]

Proof 6.1
.

Since
{\Delta}=\varnothing
, then by
Section

6
,
{\Delta}
is
fully-balanced
.
We proceed by induction on the nature of the reduction (
\,\longrightarrow^{\mbox{}}\,
):

Case 1.

If

\,\longrightarrow^{\mbox{}}\,=\,\rightsquigarrow^{\mbox{}}\,

then the thesis follows
1
.

Case 2.

If

\,\longrightarrow^{\mbox{}}\,=\,\rightharpoonup^{\mbox{}}\,

then the thesis follows
2
.

As a safety property of our typing system, subject reduction guarantees
that any action performed by a well-typed process is as prescribed by the types; i.e., is timely and correct.

7.
Related Work

This paper is an extended version of
[
PBK23
]
featuring numerous improvements and three substantial additions to the existing work:
(1) the proofs of progress for TOAST given in
Appendix

A
;
(2) the type-checking rules in
Section

5
; and,
(3) the subject reduction result in
Section

6
together with its proof in
Appendix

B
.
We have also made several changes to the syntax of processes in
Section

3
and the reduction rules in
Section

3.2
to better accommodate the new type-checking rules and our focus on subject reduction result. These changes are largely syntactic, and are equivalent to those in the original. For convenience, we report below in detail about the changes to the syntax of processes with respect to the short version of this paper. Most notably, we have simplified (without loss of generality) the definition of
{e}
from being a linear expression over process timers and numeric constants,
to either
\diamond{n}
,
\infty
or
\leq 0
, as defined in
Equation

3.1
.
For readability, we have streamlined the syntax of branching processes with and without timeout.
We have removed
\mathtt{error}
processes, as our focus is not that of showing error-freedom.

Asynchronous Timed Session Types

Since our work builds on Asynchronous Timed Session Types
[
BMVY19
]
, we begin by highlighting the differences between the two. Our work adds expressiveness to the types in
[
BMVY19
]
by allowing mixed-choice. Our processes are also extended.
The processes in
[
BMVY19
]
allow a simplified variant of a timeout where a receive action is annotated with a deadline, akin to our branching primitive
p^{{e}}\,{\MathOpPrcRecv\mathstrut}\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{{l}_{i}}{\left({{v}_{i}}\right)}:{P}_{i}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.
The added expressiveness of mixed-choice in TOAST allow us to introduce a process to execute upon timeout to express, for instance, retry strategies, which are not expressible in
[
BMVY19
]
.
In addition to timeouts we have allowed our processes to model timers. By using timing constraints over timers as conditional statements it becomes straightforward to derive the corresponding “
dual
” of a given timeout processes.

Similarly
to
[
BMVY19
]
,
type-checking using our typing system is decidable under the assumption that
channel and recursion variables
are annotated with typing information.
The rules in our typing system with infinitely many premises, such as ‘
\forall{t}\in{d}
’ in the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{Del}}\text{-}{d}}\right]

in
Equation

5.3
,
can be accounted for symbolically, such as by exploiting zones and Difference Bound Matricies
(DBMs)
[
BY03
]

or Satisfiability Modulo Theory (SMT).

In this version of our work we do not feature the
\mathtt{error}
process, which is featured in
[
BMVY19
]
and
[
PBK23
]
.
More similarly to the earlier work of Timed MPST
[
BYY14
]
, instead of letting a process reaching an error state (
\mathtt{error}
) when a time constraint is violated, we do not allow time to pass.
(See
Section

3.3
.)
This choice allows us to manage the complexity added by mixed-choice and separate the concerns of subject reduction, safety and progress.
Normally, safety and progress come as consequences of subject reduction.
Conversely, in a timed scenario with error states, subject reduction depends on progress (i.e., if a process gets stuck some time constraint may be violated, yielding an error state reached, hence compromising subject reduction).
In
[
BMVY19
]
this circular dependency was overcome by requiring a progress property called receive-liveness, which is defined on the untimed counterpart of a timed process, which can be checked by well-established techniques
[
DdY07
,
BCD+08
]
.
Unfortunately, not only do techniques such as
[
DdY07
]
not apply to mixed-choice, but they are hardly attainable in this setting:
removing time from a mixed-choice is likely to introduce progress violations that would otherwise not be there in the presence of time.
Therefore, removing
\mathtt{error}
processes became critical in order to break this circular dependency and establish subject reduction.
As a future work, we would like to establish a progress property on the basis of subject reduction.

Runtime Monitoring

Recent work by
[
PBH24
]
presented a work-in-progress toolchain for generating Erlang stub programs from a protocol specification notation derived from the theory of TOAST presented in this paper, with the aim of bridging the gap between protocol specifications and program implementations.
The toolchain generates correct-by-construction Erlang stub programs which provide a bare-bones scaffolding structure for a user to extend with functionality (i.e., what to send and how to handle receptions).

In addition to generating Erlang stub programs,
[
PBH24
]
provide a generic runtime monitoring program for Erlang, which is provided as a precautionary measure to ensure that user of the toolchain does not unintentionally introduce unspecified behaviour when extending the Erlang stubs with functionality. The monitoring program can be setup to monitor the communication of any Erlang process against a given protocol specification, and can be configured to either: (a)
verify
the process communicates as specified, or (b) try to
enforce
the specified behaviour (e.g., by delaying the sending/receiving of messages).

Other work on timeouts

Outside of session types, a similar construct to timeout processes appears in
[
BLTV22
]
, which builds upon the Temporal Process Language.
Similarly to our work,
[
BLTV22
]
extends the receiving branch processes in
[
BMVY19
]
in a manner based on the
\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
pattern in Erlang.
However
[
BLTV22
]
does not provide a typing discipline and rather focuses on characterising reliability properties in a mailbox-based communication setting.
The “
periodic recursion
” in Rate-Based Session Types
[
ICHZ23
]
,
features a similar construct where a timeout-recursive loop runs indefinitely, at a steady and fixed rate.
Instead of timing constraints on individual actions, periods are imposed on recursive loops, specifying the rate at which each iteration must adhere to, and optional deadlines which specify a fixed upper-bound.

Session types

The (untimed) Multiparty, Asynchronous and Generalised
\pi
-calculus
[
BD23
]
(MAG
\pi
)
features the option for a unique “timeout branch” in the syntax of both branching types and processes.
Notably, “timeout branches” in
[
BD23
]
are non-deterministic, and may trigger regardless of another branch being viable.
Additionally, the semantics go as far as to facilitate errors, such as by dropping unreliable messages from a queue before they can be received.
In our work, we use timeouts to model safe mixed-choice, structured in such a way that communication mismatches cannot occur.
Our work focuses on the timeliness of messages being sent and received,
and to this end our timeouts ensure only one participant can send at any time.
By comparison, the timeouts in
[
BD23
]
can also model mixed-choice,
but since they are unstructured and non-deterministic, communication mismatches may occur and as such, the type/process must be designed to handle these failures.
Our processes feature time-sensitive conditional statements in our
\mathtt{if}\text{-}\mathtt{then}\text{-}\mathtt{else}
processes to model the counterpart of a timeout, while
[
BD23
]
instead staggers timeout processes in an alternating pattern.
While this is effective in their context, it does limit the descriptive capabilities to mixed-choice with only two distinct regions of sending and receiving actions.
In comparison, our work allows mixed-choice composed of any number of sending and receiving actions interleaved together in the same state.

Fault-Tolerant MPST
[
PNW22
,
PNW23
]
allows a branching type/process to specify a default value, to be used in the case when nothing is received, abstracting from the decision procedure on when to trigger this case.
(This approach relies on an external failure detector to inform the process to take the default value.)
While different from a timeout, this does allow a process that would otherwise be stuck waiting forever, to instead make progress.
Our timeout process and timers closer align with programming primitives like timeouts and timers in e.g.,
Erlang (with its
\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
pattern and timers) and Go.

Mixed Sessions
[
VCAM20
]

introduces mixed-choice in untimed
synchronous
session types. They allow for mixed-choice with a single communication primitive, similar to our own types.
Unlike our work, choices in
[
VCAM20
]
are non-deterministic and labels in a choice do not have to be unique.
Additionally, options in a choice are either linear or
unrestricted
.
This approach leads to patterns such as the producer/consumer pattern being elegantly represented by a single type, whereas in our system several would be needed.
Effectively, the unrestricted action embeds into one of the options action within a choice, a recursive call that returns back to the choice.
This is once again similar to the timeout-recursion behaviour found in
[
BLTV22
,
ICHZ23
]
, except that
[
VCAM20
]
is
untimed
.
Recent work by
[
PY24
]

present a typing system for
mixed-choice in
synchronous
multiparty sessions
and
explores the expressiveness
offered by mixed-choice
by evaluating and comparing various other works (with and without mixed-choice).

Further afield, coordination structures have been proposed that overlap with mixed-choice, for example, fork and join
[
DY12
]
, which permit messages within a fork (and its corresponding join) to be sent or received in any order; reminiscent of mixed-choice.
Affine sessions
[
LNY22
,
MV18
]
support exception handling by enabling an end-point to perform a subset of the interactions specified by their type, but there is no consideration of time, hence timeouts.
Before session types gained traction, timed processes
[
BY07
]
were proposed for realising timeouts, but lack any notion of counterpart.

Timed semantics

Our work is based on the timed semantics of Communicating Timed Automata
[
KY06
]
(CTA).
CTA is the combined product of Timed Automata
[
AD94
]
and CFSM
[
BZ83
]
, yielding a timed asynchronous semantics.
Our time-passing semantics of
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
in
Equation

2.5
is based upon the
urgent semantics

[
BBM18
]
,
to enforce
receive urgency
and ensure that the
latest enabled
action is not missed
[
BBM18
]
.
Our semantics for time-passing over configurations differs from
[
BMVY19
]
, which requires that sending actions are never missed.
In our setting a configuration featuring a mixed-choice,
such as
({{\nu}_{0},}{\penalty\ \left\{{!\mathstrut}{a}{}\left({x<3},{\varnothing}\right).{S},{?\mathstrut}{b}{}\left({x\geq 3},{\varnothing}\right).{S^{\prime}}\right\}})

would be unable to reach the latest-enabled receiving action
b
, since the preceding sending action
a
would
always
happen instead,
effectively rendering
b
redundant.
However, in this work we only require that the latest-enabled action is never missed, allowing instances such as
a
to be passed.
This provides implementations of
TOAST protocols
the affordance of not featuring every sending action, and allows them to draw upon the full range of interactions described by the given TOAST protocol.

The work of
[
BCM17
]
presents (binary) Timed Session Types in the synchronous setting.
Instead of relying on
a
notion of
duality
,
[
BCM17
]
presents a means of determining if two parties are
compliant
, given their sets of constraints.
This allows the timing constraints on actions to be constructed without considering the dual,
and therefore, avoiding such issues as those discussed in
Section

2.1
.
E.g., if one party expects to receive
a
some time after 3 time units,
but has some proceeding action
c
that will not be enabled after a total of 5 time units,
then the party can still be guaranteed to be free from deadlocks if there is some
compliant co-party
that always sends before 4 time units;
as in
Section

2.1
.

8.
Conclusion

We have shown how timing constraints provide an intuitive way of integrating mixed-choice into asynchronous (binary) session types.
There are many conceivable ways to realise mixed-choice using programming primitives.
However, our integration with time, embodied in TOAST, offers new capabilities for modelling timeouts which sit at the heart of protocols and are a widely-used idiom in programming practice.
To provide a bridge to programming languages, we provide a timed session calculus enriched with a
\mathtt{receive}\text{-}\mathtt{after}
pattern and process timers, providing the means to implement a timeout process and its dual.

Taken altogether, we have lifted a long-standing restriction on asynchronous session types by allowing for safe mixed-choice, through the judicious application of timing constraints.
In this extended version of
[
PBK23
]
, we have established a means of type-checking our processes with TOAST.
Additionally, we have shown that processes that are well-typed by our type system adhere to subject reduction (
3
) which means that any step the system
takes
maintains
the shape of a well-typed system.
Subject reduction guarantees that when an action happens, its sender, receiver, label, payload, and timing all conform to the types.

Future Work

There are two primary focuses for future work:
(1) an extension to the multiparty setting;
and (2) a progress property for our typing system.
Progress was included in previous work on timed session types
[
BMVY19
]
. This result is not easily transferable to our work since we deal with mixed-choice.
In fact, the result in
[
BMVY19
]
builds upon existing techniques to ensure global progress
[
DdY07
]
on an untimed version of their timed processes.
In essence,
[
BMVY19
]
uses an
untimed
version of a process to check for deadlocks in the interaction structures of the process, using the checks described in
[
DdY07
]
.
We are unable to take this approach since: (a) the work in
[
DdY07
]
does not account for mixed-choice, and (b) the untimed counterpart of a
safe
timed mixed-choice may not enjoy progress since the timing constraints that make it
safe
would be removed. Proving progress in this setting requires explicit reasoning on the interactions structures.
Therefore, we will have to take a different approach to in order to provide a stronger property for our typing system.

Additionally, in future work we would like to continue the development of the theory, such as to the multiparty setting, and in turn, enable work such as the toolchain presented by
[
PBH24
]
to have a broader potential for application to real world systems.
As part of this growing toolchain, we aim to include an implementation of our typing system.

Appendix A
Proof of Type Progress

The result of
2
follows similar reasoning to
[
BMVY19
]
.
While
2
states progress for
initial system configurations
, progress is also guaranteed for systems composed of
well-formed
and
compatible
configurations by
20
.
Our proof shows that our
well-formedness
rules in
Equation

2.8
ensure that the timing-constraints on actions are
feasible
, and structured such that any mixed-choice are
safe
.
We show that our LTS in
Equations

2.4
,
2.5
and
2.6
preserve both
well-formedness
and compatibility of our configurations.

A.1.
Auxiliary Definitions & Assumptions

[Latest-enabled Configurations]

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
latest-enabled
if,
\exists\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}},\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}

such that
\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}\neq\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}

and
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}}{{\Rightarrow}}

and
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}}{{\not\Rightarrow}}
.

[Live Configurations]

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
live
if,
{S}=\mathtt{end}
or
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
future-enabled
.

[Clock Names]
We define
\mathtt{cn}({\delta})
, the
clock names
of a constraint
\delta
, as:

\mathtt{cn}({\delta})=\begin{cases}\{x\}&\text{if\ }\delta\in\begin{array}[]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}x>n,x=n\end{array}\\
\{x,y\}&\text{if\ }\delta\in\begin{array}[]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}x-y>n,x-y=n\end{array}\\
\mathtt{cn}({\delta^{\prime}})&\text{if\ }\delta=\neg\delta^{\prime}\\
\mathtt{cn}({\delta_{1}})\cup\mathtt{cn}({\delta_{2}})&\text{if\ }\delta=\delta_{1}\land\delta_{2}\\
\varnothing&\text{if\ }\delta=\MathOpTrue\end{cases}

[Free Names]
We define
\mathtt{fn}\left({S}\right)
, the
free names
of a type
{S}
, as:
3
3

3

As in
[
Pie02
]
.

\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}}\right)=\begin{cases}\{\alpha\}&\text{if\ }{S}=\alpha\\
\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}^{\prime}}\right)\setminus\{\alpha\}&\text{if\ }{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}\\
\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}_{i}}\right)&\text{if\ }{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\\
\varnothing&\text{if\ }{S}=\mathtt{end}\end{cases}

[Capture Avoiding Substitution]
Substitution
\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is
capture avoiding
for
{S}
iff:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{}}{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}&\Longrightarrow&\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\text{\ is capture avoiding for ${S}_{j}$,\ }\forall j\in I\\
{S}=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\land\alpha\neq\beta&\Longrightarrow&\beta\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}^{\prime}}\right)\text{\ and\ }\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\text{\ is capture avoiding for}{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}

With an abuse of notation, we say that
{S}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is capture avoiding when
\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is capture avoiding for
{S}
.

[Unfold Equivalence]
We define
(\UTU)
as the smallest relation such that:

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}l@{}}{S}\UTU{S}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\implies{S}^{\prime}\UTU{S}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\land{S}^{\prime}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\implies{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\\[3.87498pt]
\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}l@{}}{S}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\text{\ is capture avoiding}\implies\mu\alpha.{S}\UTU{S}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\implies\mu\alpha.{S}\UTU\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\\[3.87498pt]
\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\implies\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{\ }l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{{{l}}\left\langle{{T}}\right\rangle}({{\delta}},{{\lambda}}).{{S}},&{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}\end{array}\UTU\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{\ }l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{{{l}}\left\langle{{T}}\right\rangle}({{\delta}},{{\lambda}}).{{S}^{\prime}},&{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

If
{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails{S}
,
then
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
.

A.2.
Well-formed Types

Lemma 1
.

If
\alpha\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}}\right)

and
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
,
then

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails{S}\iff{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}
.

Proof A.1
.

Since
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
it follows that recursive call
\alpha
has not yet been defined.
Additionally, since
\alpha\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}}\right)
, then it follows
Section

A.1
that
if
\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}
appears at any point within
{S}
, then
\alpha

may
appear at some point within
{S}^{\prime}
.
For each case of (
\iff
):

Case 1.

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails{S}\Longrightarrow{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
If
{S}=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}
, then by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}

Notice the environment

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta}

in the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
(above).
If
\beta=\alpha
then, the environment would contain duplicate variables.
Therefore,
\beta\neq\alpha

or
\beta
must be renamed to some
\beta^{\prime}\notin\text{dom}({{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime}})

and
{S}=\mu\beta^{\prime}.{S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\beta^{\prime}}}{{\beta}}}\right]
.
Similarly, if
{S}=\beta
then, since
\alpha\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({\beta}\right)
, it must be that
\beta\neq\alpha
.
In both of these cases, the presence of
\alpha:{\delta}
is immediately inconsequential, and will remain so for the remainder of the evaluation.
Clearly, if
{S}=\mathtt{end}
then the same holds.
If
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
then by
Section

A.1
, it must also hold for all
i\in I
.
In summary, since
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
and
\alpha\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}}\right)
, it follows that
\alpha
must correspond to a recursive variable in a different unfolding (either outside
{S}
, or a future unfolding within
{S}
), and that it has been renamed from the corresponding recursive variable in the current unfolding.

Case 2.

{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}\Longrightarrow{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
Following
1
,
the presence of
\alpha:{\delta}
is inconsequential since
\alpha
corresponds to some already unfolded recursive call outside
{S}
, or a some future unfolding within
{S}
.
In both cases, the
\alpha
has been renamed from the corresponding recursive variable in the current unfolding.

Lemma 2

(Substitution Lemma)
.

Let
{A};{\delta}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
.
If
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
and
{S}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is
capture avoiding
, then

{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\iff{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails{S}
holds.

Proof A.2
.

We proceed by induction on the structure of
{S}
:

Case 1.

If
{S}=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}

then, for each case of (
\iff
):

i.

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\Longrightarrow{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
.

a.

If
\beta=\alpha
, then

\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\beta}}}\right]=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
.
Therefore:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}

Since
\alpha=\beta
and
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
, by
2
we have the thesis:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}

b.

If
\beta\neq\alpha
,
then by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

By
Section

A.1
,
\beta\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}^{\prime}}\right)
and
{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is
capture avoiding
.
By
1
:

{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}^{\prime};\Entails{S}^{\prime}\iff{A};{\delta}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
.
By the induction hypothesis:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\iff{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta};\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

We obtain our thesis following the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
(below), using the former case (above) as our premise:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}

ii.

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\Longrightarrow{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}

It cannot be that
\beta=\alpha
, as otherwise the environment

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta}

would contain a duplicate variable (as in the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
, above).
Therefore, it must be that
\beta\neq\alpha
.
Since
\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is
capture avoiding
, by
Section

A.1
,
\beta\notin\mathtt{fn}\left({{S}^{\prime}}\right)
and
{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is also
capture avoiding
.
By
1
:

{A};{\delta}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}\iff{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
.
By the induction hypothesis:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\iff{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

We obtain our thesis following the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
(below), using the latter case (above) as the premise:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]{A},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

Case 2.

If
{S}=\beta

then, for each case of (
\iff
):

i.

{A};{\delta}\Entails\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\Longrightarrow{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\beta
.

a.

If
\beta=\alpha
,
then
\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]={S}^{\prime}

and
{\delta}^{\prime}={\delta}
.
Since
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
, it follows:

{A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails\beta

b.

If
\beta\neq\alpha
,
then
\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]=\beta

and
{A}={A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta}

and by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
:

{A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails\beta

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
(and up-to reordering of variables):

{A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\beta

ii.

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\beta\Longrightarrow{A};{\delta}\Entails\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.

a.

If
\beta=\alpha
,
then
{\delta}^{\prime}={\delta}

and
\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]={S}^{\prime}
.
The thesis coincides with the hypothesis:

{A};{\delta}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}

b.

If
\beta\neq\alpha
,
then
\beta\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]=\beta
and

{A}={A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta}

and by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
:

{A}^{\prime},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails\beta

The thesis follows immediately by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
:

{A}^{\prime},\beta:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails\beta

Case 3.

If
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

then, for each case of (
\iff
):

i.

{A};{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\Longrightarrow{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.

By
Section

A.1
, for all
i\in I
,
{S}_{i}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is
capture avoiding
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
:

{A};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\begin{array}[]{@{}c @{\hspace{2ex}} l@{}}\forall i\in I:{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(feasibility)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i,j\in I:i\neq j\implies{\delta}_{i}\land{\delta}_{j}\models\MathOpFalse\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(mixed-choice)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i\in I:{T}_{i}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}\implies\varnothing;\penalty\ {\gamma}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(delegation)}\end{array}

(A.1)

where
{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
By the induction hypothesis:

{A};{\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\iff{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\qquad\text{where\ }{\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}

Therefore, we obtain our thesis following the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
(below), using the latter case (above) as the
(feasibility)
premise:

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\begin{array}[]{@{}c @{\hspace{2ex}} l@{}}\forall i\in I:{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};\penalty\ {\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(feasibility)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i,j\in I:i\neq j\implies{\delta}_{i}\land{\delta}_{j}\models\MathOpFalse\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(mixed-choice)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i\in I:{T}_{i}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}\implies\varnothing;\penalty\ {\gamma}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(delegation)}\end{array}

(A.2)

ii.

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\Longrightarrow{A};{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
as in
Equation

A.2
,

{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
By the
(feasibility)
premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
,
for all
i\in I
,

{A},\alpha:{\delta}^{\prime};{\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}

and
{\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}
.
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis:

{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\iff{A};{\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\qquad\text{where\ }{\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}

Our thesis follows the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
in
Equation

A.1
, where we use the latter case (above) as the
(feasibility)
premise.

Case 4.

If
{S}=\mathtt{end}

then, by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
:

{A};\MathOpTrue\Entails\mathtt{end}

where
{\delta}=\MathOpTrue
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
,
\mathtt{end}
is
well-formed
against any
{\delta}
and any
{A}
.
It follows that
\mathtt{end}\left[{\nicefrac{{{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]=\mathtt{end}
.
Therefore, the hypothesis holds for
{S}=\mathtt{end}
.

We have shown the hypothesis to hold for any
{S}
.
The interesting cases are when
{S}=\mu\beta.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
or
{S}=\beta
, which vary depending on
\alpha=\beta
or
\alpha\neq\beta
.
Since
\alpha\notin\text{dom}({{A}})
, we are dealing with either:
(1) an unfolding of a yet-to-be defined recursion
\alpha
,
or (2) an future unfolding of a renamed recursion.
By our thesis, we prove that unfolding and renaming recursive types does not interfere with the judgements of
well-formedness
found in the rules in
Equation

2.8
.

A.3.
Well-formed Configurations

Lemma 3
.

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mathtt{end}})
is always
well-formed
.

Proof A.3
.

By
Section

2.3.1
,
\exists{\delta}

such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}

and
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails\mathtt{end}
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
in
Equation

2.8
,
{\delta}=\MathOpTrue
.
Therefore, our thesis follows
{\nu}\models\MathOpTrue
.

Lemma 4
.

If
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
,
then
{S}\neq\alpha
.

Proof A.4
.

Let us consider by contradiction that
{S}=\alpha
.
By
Section

2.3.1
,
\exists{\delta}
such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}
and
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails\alpha
.
The only rule applicable to
\alpha
is
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
:

{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails\alpha

Yet,

\varnothing\neq{A},\alpha:{\delta}
,
and so
{S}=\alpha
contradicts with
Section

2.3.1
and we obtain our thesis.

Lemma 5
.

If
{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}
,
then
{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}\ \implies{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof A.5
.

We proceed by induction on the last equation of
Section

A.1
used to establish
{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}
.

Case 1.

If

{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}

with
{S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
capture avoiding,
then

{S}^{\prime}={S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.
The only rule applicable to

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}

is rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}_{0}

Note that
\alpha
cannot be in
\text{dom}({{A}})
.
Since
{S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
is capture avoiding,
by
2
:

{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}_{0}\iff{A};{\delta}\Entails{S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

The thesis follows the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
, using the latter case (above) as premise:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}_{0}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]

Case 2.

If

{S}=\mu\alpha{S}_{0}
,
then

{S}^{\prime}=\mu\alpha{S}^{\prime}_{0}

with
{S}_{0}\UTU{S}^{\prime}_{0}
.
The only rule applicable to

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}

is rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}_{0}{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}_{0}

By the induction hypothesis applied to the premise of the above:

{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}_{0}
.
Therefore, we obtain our thesis following the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

{A};{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}_{0}{A},\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}_{0}

Case 3.

If

{S}=\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}_{0}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

then

{S}^{\prime}=\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{0}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

with
{S}^{\prime}_{0}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{0}
.
(For brevity and clarity we only show for
\left\lvert I\right\rvert=1
.)
The only rule applicable to

{A};{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[c]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}_{0}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

is rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
:

{A};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}_{0}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\begin{array}[]{@{}c @{\hspace{2ex}} l@{}}{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}\Entails{S}^{\prime}_{0}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]^{\prime}\models{\gamma}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(feasibility)}\\[2.15277pt]
{T}^{\prime}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}\implies\varnothing;\penalty\ {\gamma}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(delegation)}\end{array}

where
{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}=\downarrow{{\delta}^{\prime}}
.
(Since
\left\lvert I\right\rvert=1
, we omit the
(mixed-choice)
premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
.)
By the induction hypothesis, applied to the
(feasibility)
premise above:

{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{0}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]^{\prime}\models{\gamma}

We obtain our thesis following the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
, when the above is used as the
(feasibility)
premise:

{A};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}^{\prime}}{{{l}}^{\prime}\left\langle{{T}}^{\prime}\right\rangle}({{\delta}^{\prime}},{{\lambda}^{\prime}}).{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{0}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\begin{array}[]{@{}c @{\hspace{2ex}} l@{}}{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}_{0}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]^{\prime}\models{\gamma}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(feasibility)}\\[2.15277pt]
{T}^{\prime}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}\implies\varnothing;\penalty\ {\gamma}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(delegation)}\end{array}

Lemma 6
.

If
{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}
,
then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed

\implies

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
is
well-formed
.

Proof A.6
.

By
Section

2.3.1
,
\exists{\delta}
such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}
and
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
The thesis follows immediately from
5
,
since
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7
.

If
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
,
then
{S}\UTU\mathtt{end}

or
{S}\UTU\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.

Proof A.7
.

Since
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
, by
Section

2.3.1

\exists{\delta}
such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}
and
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
We proceed by induction on the derivation, analysing the structure of
{S}
.
By
Section

A.1
, the only interesting case is
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
and
{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
:

\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}

By
2
,

\alpha:{\delta};{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}\iff\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.
Therefore, it holds that
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})
is
well-formed
, and so, we obtain our thesis by the induction hypothesis:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\penalty\ \text{is\ \emph{well-formed}}\implies({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})\penalty\ \text{is\ \emph{well-formed}}

where
{S}^{\prime}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
.
Since types are
contractive
, we rule out the possibility of
{S}^{\prime}=\mu\alpha_{1}\dots\mu\alpha_{n}.\alpha_{i}
, for some
i\leq n
where
1<n\in\MathOpSetNatural
.

A.4.
Live Configurations

Lemma 8
.

If
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
,
then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
live
.

Proof A.8

(
4
)
.

By
Section

2.3.1
,
\exists{\delta}

such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}

and
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
We proceed by induction on the derivation of
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}
by the
well-formedness
rules in
Equation

2.8
:

Case 1.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
and

{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
By
Section

2
,
\exists t
such that
{\nu}+{t}\models\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}
.
It follows
Section

2.2.1
that
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
future-enabled
.
Following
Section

A.1
we obtain our thesis.

Case 2.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{rec}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}
.
By
6
, since
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}})
is
well-formed
, if
{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})
is
well-formed
.
By
Section

A.1
,
\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}\UTU{S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]={S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, since
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})
is
well-formed
, the thesis follows by induction hypothesis on
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})
being
live
.

Case 3.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\mathtt{end}
.
By
Section

A.1
,
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mathtt{end}})
is
live
.

Case 4.

Rule
\left[{\mathtt{{var}}}\right]
is not applicable following
4
, as
{S}\neq\alpha
.

Lemma 9
.

If
({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
, then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
.

Proof A.9
.

By
7
,
{S}\UTU\mathtt{end}
or
{S}\UTU\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.
By
6
, together with rules
\left[{\mathtt{{end}}}\right]
and
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
we have that
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}

where
{\delta}=\MathOpTrue

or
{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
Obviously,
{\nu}\models\MathOpTrue
.
The fact
{\nu}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
instead follows from the definition of
\downarrow{}
.

Lemma 10
.

If
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}

and
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
future-enabled
,
then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
.

Proof A.10

(
Sections

2.2.1
and
10
)
.

By
Section

2.2.1
,
\exists{t}

s.t.

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
.
We proceed by induction on the last rule applied for the transition

({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,
of those in
Equation

2.4
:

Case 1.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
and:

({{\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right],}{\penalty\ {S}_{j}}){\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models\delta_{j}\quad{m}={{{l}}_{j}\left\langle{{T}}_{j}\right\rangle}\quad j\in I

where
\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}
and
{\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}={\nu}+{t}
.
Since
\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

by hypothesis, by rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
we conclude:

{A};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}

where
{A}=\varnothing
and
{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
By
Section

2.3.1
, it remains to show that
{\nu}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
First we show that
{\nu}+{t}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
By the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
,
{\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\delta}_{j}
(where
{\nu}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}={\nu}+{t}
).
Since
{\delta}_{j}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
, it follows that

{\nu}+{t}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}

and therefore,
({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})
is
well-formed
.
By the conclusion of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
, the set of constraints
{\delta}=\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
only require a minimum of
one weak past
constraint to be satisfied for the entire set constraint to be satisfied.
Therefore, given that we know
{\nu}+{t}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
, it follows that
{\nu}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
must also hold,
and we obtain our thesis.

Case 2.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{unfold}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
and:

({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

By the induction hypothesis we have that

({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})

is
well-formed
.
Then by
6
we have that
({{\nu}+{t},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})
is
well-formed
.
Finally, by
9
,
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})
is
well-formed
, as required.

Lemma 11
.

If
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,
,
then
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \overline{{S}\mathstrut}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\overline{{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{\mathstrut}}{m}}}}$}}\,
.

Proof A.11
.

We proceed by induction on the last rule applied for the transition
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,
of those in
Equation

2.4
:

Case 1.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
and:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right],}{\penalty\ {S}_{j}}){\nu}\models\delta_{j}\quad{m}={{{l}}_{j}\left\langle{{T}}_{j}\right\rangle}\quad j\in I

where
\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}
.
By
Section

2.3
,
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\overline{{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{\mathstrut}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}{\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle}{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{\overline{{{S}}{\mathstrut}}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
.
Therefore, as the preconditions (
{\delta}_{i}
) in
{S}
and
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}
are identical, it follows that the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
shown above is equally applicable to
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \overline{{S}\mathstrut}})
.
With the only difference being the interactions having opposite directions (sending or receiving), we obtain our thesis.

Case 2.

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{unfold}}}\right]
, then
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
and:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

where
\ell=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}
.
By
Section

2.3
,
\overline{{S}\mathstrut}=\mu\alpha.\overline{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\mathstrut}
.
The thesis follows by induction:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,\quad\implies\quad({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \overline{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\mathstrut}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.\overline{{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\mathstrut}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\overline{{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}}{\mathstrut}}{m}}}}$}}\,

A.5.
Configuration Transitions

Lemma 12
.

Let
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
be
well-formed
.

12
and
A.5
both hold.

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})\implies{{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}+{t}}\land{{S}\UTU{S}^{\prime}}

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})\implies\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\ }l@{}}\exists{\delta}&\text{s.t.\ }{\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}}\land{{\nu}\models{\delta}}\\
\mathllap{\land\ }\exists{\delta}^{\prime}&\text{s.t.\ }{{\delta}^{\prime}={\delta}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]}\land{{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}}\mapsto 0}\right]}\\
\mathllap{\land\ }\exists{\gamma}&\text{s.t.\ }{{\nu}^{\prime}\models{\delta}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}}\land{\varnothing;{\gamma}\Entails{S}^{\prime}}\end{array}

Proof A.12
.

We proceed by addressing each claim in turn:

12
:

We proceed by induction on the derivation of the transition

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

analysing the last rule applied of those given in
Equation

2.4
:

Case 1.:

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{tick}}}\right]
,
then the thesis holds as
{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}+{t}
and
{S}={S}^{\prime}
.

Case 2.:

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{unfold}}}\right]
,
then
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
and:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

where
\ell={t}
.
By
Section

A.1
,
\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\UTU{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]
.
By
6
,
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})
is
well-formed
.
By the induction hypothesis,

{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]\UTU\allowbreak{S}^{\prime}
.
By the transitivity equation in
Section

A.1
,

\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\UTU{S}^{\prime}
, as required.

Section

A.5
:

We proceed by induction on the derivation of the transition
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
, analysing the last rule applied of those given in
Equation

2.4
:

Case 1.:

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
,
then
{S}=\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}
and:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right],}{\penalty\ {S}_{j}}){\nu}\models\delta_{j}\quad{m}={{{l}}_{j}\left\langle{{T}}_{j}\right\rangle}\quad j\in I

where
{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]
,
{S}^{\prime}={S}_{j}
and
{\delta}={\delta}_{j}
.
Since
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
is
well-formed
, by
Section

2.3.1
,
\exists{\delta}
such that
{\nu}\models{\delta}
and

\varnothing;{\delta}\Entails{S}
.
By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
:

{A};\penalty\ \downarrow{\bigvee\limits_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}\Entails\begin{array}[t]{\{}{@{}l@{}}\}{\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}}\,{{l}_{i}}\left\langle{{T}_{i}}\right\rangle{\left({{{\delta}_{i}},{{\lambda}_{i}}}\right)}.{{S}_{i}}\end{array}_{i\in I}\begin{array}[]{@{}c @{\hspace{2ex}} l@{}}\forall i\in I:{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}_{i}\Entails{S}_{i}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}_{i}\left[{{{\lambda}_{i}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}_{i}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(feasibility)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i,j\in I:i\neq j\implies{\delta}_{i}\land{\delta}_{j}\models\MathOpFalse\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{i}=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}_{j}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(mixed-choice)}\\[2.15277pt]
\forall i\in I:{T}_{i}=\begin{array}[c]{(}{@{}l@{}}){\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime},\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\end{array}\implies{A};\penalty\ {\gamma}^{\prime}\Entails{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ {\delta}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\models{\gamma}^{\prime}\hfil\hskip 8.61108pt&\text{(delegation)}\end{array}

where
{A}=\varnothing
.
As in
1
of
10
, the set of constraints
\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
requires as minimum the
weak past
of only
one
set of constraints (
{\delta}_{j}
) to be satisfied for the entire set of constraints to be satisfied; hence
{\delta}_{j}\models\downarrow{\bigvee_{i\in I}{\delta}_{i}}
.
It remains to show:

{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\delta}_{j}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}

and
\varnothing;{\gamma}\Entails{S}_{j}
.
Clearly, by the
(feasibility)
premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{choice}}}\right]
it holds that
{\delta}_{j}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\gamma}
and
\varnothing;{\gamma}\Entails{S}_{j}
(as
{A}=\varnothing
).
By the premise of rule
\left[{\mathtt{{act}}}\right]
, it holds that
{\nu}\models{\delta}_{j}
, and therefore it follows that
{\nu}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]\models{\delta}_{j}\left[{{{\lambda}_{j}}\mapsto 0}\right]
.

Case 2.:

If rule
\left[{\mathtt{{unfold}}}\right]
,
then
{S}=\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}
and:

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ \mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}\left[{\nicefrac{{{\mu\alpha.{S}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}}}}{{\alpha}}}\right]})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\ell}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

where
\ell=\mbox{\tiny{$\square$}}{m}
.
The rest follows
2
of
12
(above).

Lemma 13
.

Let
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})
be
well-formed
.

13
,
A.5
,
A.5
and
A.5
all hold.

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}})\implies\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}=\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}\land({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{\tau}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}})\implies\exists{m}\penalty\ \text{s.t.\ }\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}={m};\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}\land({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}})\implies{S}^{\prime}={S}\land\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}=\mathbf{\mathtt{M}};{m}\land{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}

({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{{t}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ \mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}})\implies{S}^{\prime}\UTU{S}\land\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}^{\prime}=\mathbf{\mathtt{M}}\land{\nu}^{\prime}={\nu}+{t}

Proof A.13
.

We proceed addressing each claim in turn, using the rules in
Equation

2.5
:

13
:

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{send}}}\right]
the claim holds.
By the premise
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{!{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
.

Section

A.5
:

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{recv}}}\right]
the claim holds.
By the premise
({{\nu},}{\penalty\ {S}})\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[12.5pt]{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rule{0.0pt}{4.52083pt}{?{m}}}}$}}\,({{\nu}^{\prime},}{\penalty\ {S}^{\prime}})
.

Section

A.5
:

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{que}}}\right]
the claim holds.

Section

A.5
:

By rule
\left[{\mathtt{{time}}}\right]
the claim holds.
By the premise

via rule
\left[{\mathtt{{tick}}}\right]
.
(See
